Characterization of debris disks observed with SPHERE
N. Engler1, J. Milli2, N. Pawellek3, 4, R. Gratton5, P. Thébault6, C. Lazzoni5, J. Olofsson7, H. M. Schmid1,
S. Ulmer-Moll8, C. Perrot6, J.-C. Augereau2, S. Desidera5, G. Chauvin9, M. Janson10, C. Xie11, Th. Henning9,
A. Boccaletti6, S. B. Brown-Sevilla9, E. Choquet12, C. Dominik13, C. Ginski14, A. Zurlo15, 16, M. Feldt9, T. Fusco17, 12,
J. H. Girard18, D. Gisler19, R. G. van Holstein20, A.-M. Lagrange6, 2, M. Langlois12, 21, A.-L. Maire2, D. Mesa5,
P. Rabou2, L. Rodet22, M. Samland9, T. Schmidt6, and A. Vigan12
(Affiliations can be found after the references)
Received: 1 April 2025; Accepted: 9 October 2025
ABSTRACT
Aims. This study aims to characterize debris disk targets observed with SPHERE across multiple programs, with the goal of identifying systematic
trends in disk morphology, dust mass, and grain properties as a function of stellar parameters. By combining scattered-light imaging with photo-
metric and parametric modeling, we seek to improve our understanding of the composition and evolution of circumstellar material in young debris
systems and to place debris disks in the broader context of planetary system architectures.
Methods. We analyzed a sample of 161 young main-sequence stars using archival SPHERE observations at optical and near-infrared (IR) wave-
lengths. Disk geometries were derived from ellipse fitting and model grids, while dust mass and properties were constrained by modified blackbody
(MBB) and size distribution (SD) modeling of spectral energy distributions (SEDs). We also carried out dynamical modeling to assess whether the
observed disk structures can be explained by the presence of unseen planets.
Results. We resolve 51 debris disks, including four new detections where disks are resolved for the first time: HD 36968, BD-20 951, and the inner
belts of HR 8799 and HD 36546. In addition, we find a second transiting giant planet in the HD 114082 system, with a radius of 1.29 ± 0.05 RJup
and an orbital distance of ∼1 au, providing an important new benchmark for planet–disk interaction studies.
Beyond these new detections, we identify nine multi-belt systems, with outer-to-inner belt radius ratios of 1.5−2, and find close agreement between
scattered-light and millimeter continuum belt radii with a mean ratio Rbelt(near-IR)/Rbelt(mm) of 1.05 ± 0.04. Belt radii scale weakly with stellar
luminosity (Rbelt ∝L0.11±0.05
⋆
), but show steeper dependencies when separated by CO and CO2 freeze-out regimes, and also increase with age as
Rbelt ∝t0.37±0.11
age
.
Uniform image modeling yields vertical disk aspect ratios of 0.02 −0.06, consistent with collisionally stirred belts, while gas-rich systems show
unusually small values. Inner density slopes steepen with stellar luminosity, indicating more efficient dust removal around luminous stars.
Disk fractional luminosities follow collisional decay trends, declining as t−1.18±0.14
age
for A-type and t−0.81±0.12
age
for F-type stars. SD modeling yields
minimum grain sizes consistently above the blowout limit, typically > 0.8 µm, with a mean SD index of q = 3.6, assuming astrosilicate composi-
tion. The inferred dust masses span 10−5 −1 M⊕from MBB modeling (and 0.01 −1 M⊕from SD modeling for detected disks). These masses scale
as Rn
belt with n > 2 in belt radius and super-linearly with stellar mass, consistent with trends seen in protoplanetary disks (PPDs).
Our detailed analysis of disk scattered-light non-detections indicates that they are mainly caused by low dust masses, unfavorable viewing ge-
ometries, or suboptimal observing conditions. SD modeling combined with Mie theory further shows that bulk albedos are consistently above 0.5
with little variation, making albedo differences an unlikely explanation. To explore this further, we introduced a new parametric approach based
on scattered-light and polarized-light images, which provides independent estimates of dust albedo and maximum polarization fraction.
We find a correlation between measured disk polarized flux and IR excess, with a slope shallower than that of optical total-intensity fluxes measured
with HST/STIS. The offset of ∼1 dex between total-intensity and polarized fluxes arises because polarized flux represents only a fraction of the
total scattered light which depends on both grain properties and disk inclination.
Finally, a comparison of planetary architectures shows that most benchmark systems resemble the Solar System, with multiple planets located
inside wide Kuiper-belt analogues. Dynamical modeling further indicates that many observed gaps and inner edges can be explained by unseen
planets below current detection thresholds, typically with Neptune- to sub-Jovian masses, underscoring the likely ubiquity of such planets in
shaping debris disk morphologies.
Key words. interplanetary medium – planets and satellites: detection – planet–disk interactions
1. Introduction
1
The field of exoplanet research, which has rapidly evolved in re-
2
cent decades, has uncovered an immense diversity in planetary
3
structure and composition, ranging from small rocky worlds to
4
massive gas giants, orbiting their stars in periods spanning days
5
to hundreds of years (Jontof-Hutter 2019; Winn & Fabrycky
6
2015; Dawson & Johnson 2018; Zhu & Dong 2021; Wordsworth
7
& Kreidberg 2022, and references therein). The vast variety of
8
exoplanets might arise from the distinct environments of circum-
9
stellar gas and dust in which planets form and evolve over mil-
10
lions, or even billions, of years. These environments undergo
11
continuous transformations: beginning with the collapse of a
12
molecular cloud that gives rise to a new star, progressing through
13
a protoplanetary disk (PPD), where planets are born, and eventu-
14
ally becoming a debris disk as the star enters the main sequence
15
after several million years. Studying these environments is es-
16
sential to answering fundamental questions in exoplanet science.
17
The circumstellar material that provides the building blocks
18
for future planets has different origins and properties in proto-
19
planetary and debris disks. In PPDs, gas and dust are pristine,
20
originating directly from the initial molecular cloud. In contrast,
21
the primary mechanism of dust production in debris disks is
22
Article number, page 1 of 57


## Page 2


A&A proofs: manuscript no. output
the collisions between kilometer-sized rocky bodies. These col-
23
lisions supply the disk and the forming planets with substantial
24
amounts of dust grains of various sizes and small amounts of gas
25
(e.g., Wyatt 2018; Hughes et al. 2018).
26
The evolution of dust particle properties from the protoplan-
27
etary to the debris disk phase can be studied using two distinct
28
ranges of the electromagnetic spectrum, as dust particles inter-
29
act with stellar light in two primary ways. Some stellar photons
30
are scattered by dust grains in all directions, particularly at opti-
31
cal and near-infrared (IR) wavelengths. Meanwhile, other stellar
32
photons are absorbed by the dust grains and re-emitted as ther-
33
mal radiation predominantly in the IR to millimeter wavelength
34
range.
35
In the past decades, space-based mid-IR and far-IR observa-
36
tions with the Spitzer, IRAS, and Herschel Space Observatory
37
played a crucial role in advancing our understanding of debris
38
disks. In particular, the Disc Emission via a Bias-free Reconnais-
39
sance in the IR/Submillimeter (DEBRIS; Sibthorpe et al. 2018)
40
and DUst around NEarby Stars (DUNES; Eiroa et al. 2013) sur-
41
veys provided comprehensive statistical studies of debris disks
42
around nearby main-sequence stars. These surveys enabled pre-
43
cise measurements of IR excess and dust temperatures, reveal-
44
ing trends with stellar type and age. They also established a
45
framework for estimating dust luminosity distributions and the
46
incidence rate of debris disks, especially around solar-type and
47
early-type stars.
48
In addition to mid-IR and far-IR observations, the Hubble
49
Space Telescope (HST) made a groundbreaking contribution to
50
the imaging of debris disks in scattered light. Using its high-
51
contrast imaging (HCI) capabilities, HST provided the first re-
52
solved views of numerous debris disks, revealing their morphol-
53
ogy and fine structures such as rings, warps, and asymmetries
54
(e.g., Golimowski et al. 2006; Kalas et al. 2007). Systematic sur-
55
veys of circumstellar environments led by Schneider et al. (2014,
56
2016) offered valuable complementary insights to thermal emis-
57
sion data, helping to constrain disk geometries and the scattering
58
properties of dust grains.
59
The detailed studies of both scattered and thermal light from
60
circumstellar disks using the ground-based telescopes became
61
possible with the start of operation of high-contrast and high-
62
resolution instruments such as the Spectro-Polarimetic High
63
contrast imager for Exoplanets REsearch (SPHERE; Beuzit et al.
64
2019) at VLT, the Gemini Planet Imager (GPI; Macintosh et al.
65
2014) or the Subaru Coronagraphic Extreme Adaptive Optics
66
(SCExAO; Jovanovic et al. 2015), along with interferometric fa-
67
cilities like the Atacama Large (sub)Millimeter Array (ALMA)
68
which delivered unprecedented images of many protoplanetary
69
and debris disks around young stars (e.g., Perrot et al. 2016; An-
70
drews et al. 2018; Avenhaus et al. 2018; Boccaletti et al. 2020;
71
Columba et al. 2024). These observations have targeted both in-
72
dividual disks (e.g., Garufi et al. 2016; Milli et al. 2017b; Olofs-
73
son et al. 2018; Ménard et al. 2020) and large disk samples (e.g.,
74
Ansdell et al. 2017; Ginski et al. 2024; Garufi et al. 2024; Matrà
75
et al. 2025), facilitating the first demographic studies that address
76
the morphology of these objects.
77
Most optical, near-IR and (sub)millimeter imaging cam-
78
paigns have focused on studying PPD evolution and searching
79
for forming planets within them (Benisty et al. 2023, and refer-
80
ences therein). In contrast, only a few comparable studies have
81
investigated direct imaging (DI) data for a large sample of debris
82
disks (e.g., Schneider et al. 2014; Esposito et al. 2020; Crotts
83
et al. 2024). One key reason is that debris disks are significantly
84
older (≳7 Myr) and contain roughly three orders of magnitude
85
less dust than PPDs (Wyatt 2008). As a result, they are much
86
fainter and more challenging to image directly.
87
This study focuses on SPHERE observations of debris disks.
88
SPHERE is an extreme adaptive optics (AO) instrument opti-
89
mized for observing circumstellar environments. Since its com-
90
missioning in 2014, SPHERE has been extensively utilized and
91
has proven to be one of the most productive HCI instruments.
92
As part of the SPHERE guaranteed time observation (GTO) pro-
93
gram, numerous debris disks have been observed and detected in
94
the course of the dedicated disk program, and sometimes as a by-
95
product of the SpHere INfrared survey for Exoplanets (SHINE;
96
Chauvin et al. 2017; Desidera et al. 2021; Vigan et al. 2021; Lan-
97
glois et al. 2021). To perform a comprehensive analysis of these
98
observations, we compiled a sample of targets known to host de-
99
bris disks from the archival datasets of GTO and various open-
100
time programs, including all targets from the SPHERE High An-
101
gular Resolution Debris Disk Survey (SHARDDS, PI: J. Milli;
102
Milli et al. 2017b; Dahlqvist et al. 2022).
103
This study aims to consistently characterize the structural
104
and compositional properties of debris disks, focusing on both
105
their radial and vertical extents, as well as the nature of their
106
constituent dust. A primary goal is to explore how the architec-
107
ture of debris disks, particularly the radial locations of planetes-
108
imal belts and their dust masses, which are key to understanding
109
the evolution of debris disks and their interaction with planets
110
(Krivov & Wyatt 2021), relates to the fundamental properties
111
of their host stars. By analyzing a large sample of spatially re-
112
solved debris disks, we investigated how the belt radii and disk
113
dust masses scale with stellar luminosity and mass, and how
114
these relationships evolve over time. Additionally, we assessed
115
the conditions that distinguish detected from non-detected disks
116
in scattered light, accounting for both intrinsic disk properties
117
and observational biases. We further investigated the architecture
118
of planetary systems within the sample, analyzing how the pres-
119
ence, absence, or configuration of planetary companions corre-
120
lates with the structure and detectability of debris disks.
121
The paper is organized as follows. In Sect. 2, we present the
122
stellar parameters of the targets included in our sample. Section 3
123
describes the SPHERE observing modes and the data reduction
124
techniques used throughout the study. In Sect. 4, we analyze the
125
morphological structure of the detected disks, emphasizing the
126
radial locations of the planetesimal belts. These constraints are
127
then incorporated into spectral energy distribution (SED) mod-
128
eling in Sect. 5, allowing us to derive the parameters of the dust
129
grain size distributions (SDs) and to estimate plausible ranges
130
for the scattering albedo.
131
A key part of our investigation, detailed in Sect. 6, addresses
132
the question of why the majority of young debris disks remain
133
undetected in scattered light imaging. In particular, we exam-
134
ine the role of the dust’s optical properties and the observa-
135
tional biases associated with viewing geometry and disk struc-
136
ture. Special focus is placed on the evaluation methods for the
137
dust albedo and polarization efficiency based on polarimetric
138
imaging (Sect. 6.3).
139
In Sect. 7, we shift focus to planetary system architectures.
140
We analyze systems where both exoplanets and debris disks are
141
detected, as well as those with no detected planets, by estimating
142
the locations and masses of planets that could dynamically shape
143
the observed disk structures. This includes modeling scenarios in
144
which unseen planets are responsible for clearing gaps or trun-
145
cating the inner edges of planetesimal belts. The key findings of
146
the study are summarized in Sect. 8.

2. Sample description
148
We compiled a sample of debris disks from archival SPHERE
149
observations, selecting main-sequence stars with an IR excess
150
above 10−6, based on data from the Jena debris disk database1.
151
This sample comprises 161 stars spanning a broad range of spec-
152
tral types and ages. The majority are young F-type (35%) and A-
153
type (29%) stars (Fig. 1). These targets usually exhibit strong IR
154
excesses, indicating significant amounts of dust, and are bright
155
enough to serve as reference stars themselves for the AO sys-
156
tem. For instance, ZIMPOL observations require a reference star
157
with a G magnitude brighter than 9.5m for a good AO correc-
158
tion in the optical. Additionally, the two surveys that contributed
159
most to our sample, SHINE and SHARDDS, primarily focus on
160
A-type and solar-type stars. The presence of an IR excess or de-
161
bris disk was not a part of the selection criteria for the SHINE
162
statistical sample (Desidera et al. 2021). However, in specific
163
cases, the known presence of exoplanets or a disk, suggesting a
164
higher likelihood of harboring young, directly imageable plan-
165
ets (e.g., Meshkat et al. 2017), led to a target being classified
166
as a special object, thereby increasing its observational prior-
167
ity. In contrast, SHARDDS was a dedicated debris disk sur-
168
vey, with targets selected based on the predicted brightness of
169
their disks ( fdisk > 10−4)2. The SHARDDS survey included 55
170
main-sequence stars observable from the Southern hemisphere,
171
covering spectral types A through M and stellar ages ranging
172
from 10 Myr to 6 Gyr. Its aim was to provide a comprehensive
173
overview of planetary system properties and their temporal evo-
174
lution.
175
In addition to A- and solar-type stars, our sample includes
176
eight B-type and eight M-type stars, with the latter group ex-
177
hibiting the highest detection rate among all spectral types in
178
our sample. However, this high detection rate is in part due to
179
the unexpected discovery of a debris disk around the M1Ve star
180
GSC 7396-0759 (Sissa et al. 2018), which was not previously
181
known to exhibit an IR excess and was routinely observed within
182
the SHINE program. The median stellar mass of the full sample
183
is 1.43 M⊙, while for the subsample of targets with detected disks
184
it is slightly lower at 1.38 M⊙(Fig. 1).
185
The sample comprises 18 binary systems, including spectro-
186
scopic, visual, and astrometric binaries, as well as spectroscopic
187
binary candidates (SBCs), four triple systems, three quadruple
188
systems and two systems with higher-order multiplicity (N > 4)
189
according to the Washington Double Star catalog (WDS; Mason
190
et al. 2001) as of January 15, 2024. In two of the triple systems,
191
debris disks are known around two different components, which
192
were observed individually and listed separately in Table E.2.
193
These include HD 216956 (Fomalhaut A) and GSC 06964-1226
194
(Fomalhaut C), as well as HD 181296 (A component), which
195
shares a common proper motion with HD 181327 (B compo-
196
nent). Among the quadruple systems, HD 20320 consists of a
197
spectroscopic binary (SB) as its A component and an astromet-
198
ric binary as its B component, while HD 98800 features a pair of
199
SBs orbiting each other (Kennedy et al. 2019). Another quadru-
200
ple star system in the sample is HD 102647 (Denebola). The
201
components of multiple systems that host debris disks and were
202
observed with SPHERE are specified in Col. 6 of Table E.2.
203
Our sample includes five chemically peculiar stars classi-
204
fied as Lambda Boo stars: HD 30422, HD 31295, HD 110411,
205
HD 183324, and HD 218396. These stars exhibit surface defi-
206
1 https://www.physik.uni-jena.de/21956/catalog-of-resolved-debris-
disks
2 In Appendix G, we list all the symbols used in this work and provide
their definitions.
ciencies in iron-peak elements while maintaining nearly solar
207
abundances of carbon, nitrogen, oxygen, and sulfur (e.g., Paun-
208
zen 2001; Gray et al. 2017). This anomaly may be explained by
209
preferential gas accretion over dust from a dynamically evolving
210
debris disk, possibly influenced by migrating planets or accre-
211
tion from the atmospheres of hot Jupiters (Murphy & Paunzen
212
2017). The debris disk hypothesis is further supported by the
213
high fraction (up to 77%) of Lambda Boo stars exhibiting IR
214
excess, which is often linked to the presence of a debris disk.
215
(Draper et al. 2016b). Some of these disks have been imaged
216
with the Herschel Space Observatory at 70, 100 and 160 µm,
217
including several debris disks analyzed in this study (Su et al.
218
2009; Draper et al. 2016b). In Sect. 4, we present a scattered
219
light image of the inner belt surrounding a Lambda Boo star
220
HD 218396 (HR 8799).
221
Stellar ages were compiled from the literature, with their
222
lower and upper boundaries listed in Col. 11 of Table E.2. For
223
some targets, particularly field stars, there are significant dis-
224
crepancies, up to 3000 Myr, between ages reported in different
225
studies. This large scatter arises from the use of diverse age-
226
dating techniques, such as isochrone fitting, kinematic group
227
membership, and indicators of stellar activity (e.g., Ca II H and
228
K line strength or X-ray luminosity). For instance, published age
229
estimates for HD 15115 include 12+8
−4 Myr (Moór et al. 2006),
230
100 Myr (Zuckerman & Song 2004), or 500+1500
−500 Myr (Holm-
231
berg et al. 2009).
232
In such cases, we adopted an age range that covers the full
233
span of results derived from various methods. For bona fide
234
members of moving groups (MGs) and targets lacking literature
235
age estimates, upper and lower age limits were assigned based
236
on the most probable MG membership. In Column 12 of Ta-
237
ble E.2, we list the MG with the highest probability of associa-
238
tion for each star, along with the corresponding probability per-
239
centage (in parentheses), as determined using the BANYAN P
240
tool (Gagné et al. 2018). According to this analysis, the majority
241
of our sample consists of field stars (50%), followed by members
242
of the β Pictoris MG (βPMG, 9%).
243
The median age of our sample is 100 Myr, with approxi-
244
mately half of the targets estimated to be between 10 and 100
245
Myr old (Fig. 1). The debris disks around the youngest stars
246
(< 10 Myr) such as Herbig Ae/Be stars HD 141569 (e.g., Per-
247
rot et al. 2016) and HD 156623, as well as T Tauri stars such
248
as TWA 7 (e.g., Olofsson et al. 2018; Ren et al. 2021), exhibit
249
structures with multiple rings and spiral arms (Figs. 2 and B.1),
250
features typically associated with PPDs. The fractional IR lumi-
251
nosities of these young stellar objects are generally below 0.1,
252
leading to their classification as debris disks. However, these
253
systems may represent an intermediate stage between the pro-
254
toplanetary and debris disk phases. We categorize such disks as
255
transition disks due to their evolutionary status.
256
Furthermore, transition disks often contain high CO masses,
257
comparable to those found in PPDs (Lieman-Sifry et al. 2016;
258
Moór et al. 2017, 2019). Our sample includes several debris
259
disk systems with a significant gas reservoir, commonly referred
260
to as hybrid disks: HD 9672 (Moór et al. 2011; Choquet et al.
261
2017; Pawellek et al. 2019), HD 21997 (Kóspál et al. 2013),
262
HD 121617 (Perrot et al. 2023), HD 131488 (Pawellek et al.
263
2024), HD 131835 (Hung et al. 2015; Feldt et al. 2017), and
264
HD 141569 (Dent et al. 2005). In these hybrid systems, dust evo-
265
lution may have progressed more rapidly than gas dissipation
266
(Péricaud et al. 2017).
267
Similar to stellar ages, a wide range of metallicity values
268
for the same star can be found in the literature. Depending on
269
the method used to determine metallicity, discrepancies of up

3. SPHERE observing modes and data reduction
283
The disk observations presented in this work were performed
284
with different SPHERE subsystems (see Table 1 and the
285
SPHERE User Manual4): the InfraRed Dual-beam Imager and
286
Spectrograph (IRDIS; Dohlen et al. 2008), the Integral Field
287
Spectrograph (IFS; Claudi et al. 2008) and the Zurich Imaging
288
POLarimeter (ZIMPOL; Schmid et al. 2018). A variety of instru-
289
ment modes were used, including pupil- and field-stabilized con-
290
figurations, along with different filters ranging from optical to
291
near-IR. Observations were conducted with classical or apodized
292
pupil Lyot coronagraphs (Boccaletti et al. 2008; Carbillet et al.
293
2011; Guerri et al. 2011), or in some cases, without a corona-
294
graph.
295
Many disks were observed only once using either classi-
296
cal or polarimetric imaging (de Boer et al. 2020; van Holstein
297
et al. 2020) modes of IRDIS with the broadband H filter (λc =
298
1.625 µm, ∆λ = 0.290 µm), or polarimetric imaging mode of
299
ZIMPOL (Schmid et al. 2018), often employing the Very Broad
300
Band filter (VBB or RI; λc = 0.735 µm, ∆λ = 0.290 µm). The
301
observations of all SHINE targets were performed in either the
302
IRDIFS or IRDIFS_EXT modes (Langlois et al. 2021), which
303
provide a simultaneous data acquisition with both IRDIS and
304
IFS. With these instrument setups, the IRDIS is operated in the

a spectral resolution of Rλ = 50), or IRDIFS_EXT Y-H mode
312
(0.95 −1.65 µm, with a spectral resolution of Rλ = 35).
313
The IRDIS and IFS datasets were processed at the High-
314
Contrast Data Center5 (HC-DC, Delorme et al. 2017, formerly
315
known as the SPHERE Data Center). For both instruments, the
316
pre-processing steps are based on the SPHERE Data Reduction
317
and Handling pipeline (Pavlov et al. 2008) to correct for bad pix-
318
els, flat-field non-uniformity, optical distortions, and telescope or
319
sky background. In addition, for the IFS, the pre-processing in-
320
cludes a wavelength calibration and a correction for cross-talks
321
between spectral channels. Coronagraphic images are centered
322
via four satellite spots used to determine the accurate position of
323
the star hidden behind the coronagraphic mask.
324
Pre-processed IRDIS and IFS datasets form spectral and
325
temporal cubes of centered images, to which dedicated stel-
326
lar subtraction algorithms can be applied. Such algorithms in-
327
clude classical Angular Differential Imaging (ADI; Marois et al.
328
2006), Principal Component Analysis (PCA; Soummer et al.
329
2012; Amara & Quanz 2012) or the Locally Optimized Combi-
330
nation of Images (LOCI; Lafrenière et al. 2007), implemented in
331
the HC-DC in a template-oriented version (T-LOCI; Marois et al.
332
2014; Galicher et al. 2018). For several datasets, post-processing
333
employing the reference-star differential imaging (RDI) tech-
334
nique was also applied, as described in Xie et al. (2022).
335
The IRDIS polarimetric datasets were processed using the
336
IRDAP pipeline (van Holstein et al. 2020), while the ZIMPOL
337
polarimetric datasets were reduced with a pipeline developed at
338
ETH Zürich, as described in Engler et al. (2017) and Hunziker
339
et al. (2020). Both pipelines are currently implemented in the
340
HC-DC and include, as part of the pre-processing steps, sub-
341
traction of bias and dark frames, flat-fielding, and correction for
342
instrumental polarization. Additionally, the ZIMPOL frames are
343
corrected for modulation and demodulation efficiency (Schmid
344
et al. 2018).
345
In both pipelines, the Stokes parameter Q and U images are
346
computed from the calibrated and centered polarimetric frames
347
using the double-difference method. These Q and U images
348
are then transformed into the azimuthal Stokes parameter Qφ
349
and Uφ:
350
Qφ = −Q cos 2φ −U sin 2φ
351
352
Uφ = Q sin 2φ −U cos 2φ,
353
5 https://hc-dc.cnrs.fr
Article number, page 4 of 57


## Page 5


Engler et al.: Debris Disks demographics
where φ is the polar angle measured east of north in a coordinate
354
system centered on the star, and the sign convention Qφ = −Qr
355
and Uφ = −Ur is adopted from Schmid et al. (2006) (see also
356
Monnier et al. (2019)).
357
4. Morphology of resolved debris belts
358
Out of 161 targets, 51 debris disks were successfully detected
359
with SPHERE in total intensity of scattered light (Fig. 2), lin-
360
early polarized intensity6 (Fig. 5), or both. Four of these de-
361
bris disks, BD-20 951 (Perrot et al. in prep.), HD 36968 and
362
the inner belts of HD 218396 (HR 8799) and HD 36546 sys-
363
tems had not been imaged with any instrument before. Debris
364
disks HD 38206, HD 36546, HD 38397 (Perrot et al. in prep.),
365
HD 98800, HD 182681 are resolved in scattered light for the first
366
time. Scattered light images of the HD 105, HD 377, TWA 25
367
(Langlois et al. in prep.), HD 30447, HD 92945, HD 145560,
368
HD 192758 and HD 202917 debris disks have previously been
369
obtained with instruments such as HST or GPI. However, the
370
SPHERE images of these disks have not yet been published. The
371
majority of detections were around F-type stars, with 23 discov-
372
eries, corresponding to a 45% detection rate in our sample (Fig. 1
373
upper left panel).
374
We determined the radii of the resolved debris belts by ana-
375
lyzing the r2-scaled images of total or polarized intensities. The
376
radial position of the peak surface brightness (SB) along the
377
disk’s major axis was measured, and the resulting disk radius,
378
referred to as R mes
belt , is listed in Col. 2 of Table 2. The inclina-
379
tion and position angle (PA) of each disk, provided in Table 2,
380
were derived by fitting ellipses to the visible contours of the disk
381
rims. Additionally, disk images with a higher signal-to-noise ra-
382
tio (S/N) were modeled in more detail to obtain the fundamental
383
geometrical and scattering parameters necessary for a compre-
384
hensive characterization of disk properties (Sect. 4.5.1).
385
In all images presented in Figs. 2 and 5, sky north is up and
386
east to the left. The PA defines the orientation of the disk’s major
387
axis on the sky and is measured counterclockwise from sky north
388
to east. The PA values of the eastern disk extensions are listed in
389
Col. 4 of Table 2 (0◦⩽PA ⩽180◦). The inclination of a debris
390
disk is conventionally defined as the angle between the sky plane
391
and the disk’s minor axis, where a pole-on disk has an inclination
392
of 0◦, and an edge-on disk has an inclination of 90◦. In this study,
393
disk inclinations (Col. 3 of Table 2) follow the convention that
394
an inclination is less than 90◦when the brighter side of the disk
395
is oriented southward, whereas an inclination is greater than 90◦
396
when the brighter side is oriented northward. In many debris disk
397
studies, the inclination is reported as less than 90◦, regardless of
398
the orientation of the disk’s brighter side. To enable comparison
399
with such studies, the inclinations of disks with the brighter side
400
oriented northward (i > 90◦in Table 2) can be converted using
401
i<90◦= 180◦−i>90◦.
402
4.1. Disk radii in SPHERE versus ALMA observations
403
We detect planetesimal belts in 33 debris disks which have
404
also been resolved with ALMA and SMA at wavelengths of
405
0.856 −1.34 mm as part of the REASONS survey (Matrà et al.
406
2025). The nature of dust emission observed in scattered light
407
images (from optical to near-IR wavelengths) and thermal emis-
408
sion images (from mid-IR to millimeter wavelengths) is funda-
409
mentally different. In SPHERE images (both total and polarized
410
intensities), we observe stellar photons scattered off dust grains
411
into our line of sight. In contrast, the thermal emission detected
412
by ALMA and SMA originates from the absorption of stellar
413
photons by dust particles, which raises their temperature and
414

420

421
Since the spatial resolution of many millimeter observations
422
is sufficiently high to examine the relationship between belt
423
radii measured in both near-IR and millimeter wavelengths, we
424
analyze this correlation and present our results in Fig. 3. For
425
this comparison, we used disk radii measured from r2-scaled
426
SPHERE images (Col. 2 in Table 2), while the belt radii ob-
427
served in thermal emission were obtained from the REASONS
428
survey (Tables 1 and A.1 in Matrà et al. 2025). In that study,
429
all targets are fitted with a single planetesimal belt model, where
430
the radial surface density of particles is described by a Gaussian
431
distribution. Consequently, the derived belt radii represent the
432
centroid radii of this distribution.
433
To ensure consistency, we excluded from this comparison the
434
REASONS targets that were only marginally resolved in mil-
435
limeter observations or exhibited more than one planetesimal
436
belt, with two exceptions:
437
– HD 15115: The radial locations of its two cold belts were
438
taken from the two-belt model fit of the ALMA image pre-
439
sented by MacGregor et al. (2019).
440
– HD 92945: The SB profile of the disk, as shown in Fig. 2
441
by Marino et al. (2019), was used to determine the radial
442
positions of its two belts in ALMA images.
443
Figure 3 demonstrates a good agreement between the belt radii
444
measured from SPHERE and ALMA images, indicating a near
445
1:1 relationship between the locations of the radial SB peaks
446
in near-IR scattered light and thermal emission images. A lin-
447
ear fit to the data (black solid line in Fig. 3) yields a slope of
448
1.05±0.04, representing the ratio R mes
belt (near-IR)/Rbelt(mm). This
449
value is lower than the average ratio of 1.39 reported by Espos-
450
ito et al. (2020) in a similar comparison. This finding highlights
451
the need for higher-sensitivity and higher-resolution observa-
452
tions to better understand the connection between disk structures
453
observed in scattered light and thermal emission.
454
4.2. Ratio of radii in multiple belt systems
455
Observations across multiple wavelengths, from optical to mil-
456
limeter, suggest that many young debris disks likely consist of
457
multiple planetesimal belts (e.g., Golimowski et al. 2006; Bon-
458
nefoy et al. 2017; Marino et al. 2019). This is further supported
459
by the fact that many disk SEDs are better modeled using two
460
blackbody (BB) components with distinct equilibrium temper-
461
atures (Tbb), requiring dust populations at different radial dis-
462
tances from the host star7 (e.g., Chen et al. 2014).
463
7 Note, however, that caution is required when interpreting SED-
derived double belts. It has been demonstrated that when a significant
population of submicron grains is present, as expected in bright and
highly collisional debris disks, the SED of a single belt disk can mimic

Multiple belt configurations are rarely detected in DI, as
464
disks with high inclinations are more easily resolved, as dis-
465
cussed in Sect. 6.2. Among the debris disks imaged with
466
SPHERE, excluding transition disks such as HD 141569, seven
467
systems exhibit spatially resolved double-belt structures, while
468
HD 131835 (Feldt et al. 2017) and TWA 7 images reveal three
469
distinct planetesimal belts. In these systems, both the inner and
470
outer belts belong to the category of cold exo-Kuiper belts
471
(Sect. 4.3) and are listed separately in Table 2. Interestingly, the
472
ratio between the outer and inner belt radii is consistently around
473
1.5 or 2, with a median value of 1.69 across the nine resolved
474
systems (Fig. 4). These similar belt spacing ratios may hint at
475
similar evolutionary pathways of debris systems or could indi-
476
cate the presence of mean-motion resonances, possibly due to
477
unseen planets shaping these structures.
478
4.3. Empirical correlation between belt radius and star
479
luminosity
480
As mentioned in Sect. 4.2, the SEDs of many debris disks re-
481
quire a two-BB model fit. In such two-temperature debris disks,
482
the dust populations are typically classified into warm dust belts
483
(belts with BB temperature between ∼100 and 200 K) and cold
484
dust belts (exo-Kuiper belts with BB temperature below 100 K).
485
This bi-modal temperature distribution has been investigated in
486
previous studies (e.g., Kennedy & Wyatt 2014) and is often ex-
487
plained by the preferential formation of planetesimals at the ice
488
lines of volatile compounds such as water, ammonia, carbon
489
dioxide, or carbon monoxide (Morales et al. 2011).
490
that of a double belt system, with temperature ratios between the two
belts reaching up to a factor of 2 (Thebault & Kral 2019).
The ice line, also referred to as the frost or snow line, of
491
a volatile compound marks the minimum radial distance from
492
a star at which the temperature is sufficiently low for the com-
493
pound to condense. Beyond this distance, gas condensation pro-
494
motes the formation and growth of icy dust particles, thereby
495
facilitating the development of planetesimals. Consequently, de-
496
bris belts may be more likely to form just beyond the ice lines of
497
common volatile compounds such as H2O, CO or CO2.
498
The positions of ice lines within a disk are not fixed through-
499
out a star’s lifetime, as they are influenced by the evolving stellar
500
luminosity and the opacity of surrounding material. As a result,
501
the disk’s radial temperature profile and the condensation thresh-
502
olds of different volatile substances change over time. This im-
503
plies that the range of radial distances at which a specific volatile
504
compound may condense into ice can be relatively broad. For
505
example, in the solar nebula, the water snow line has been pre-
506
dicted to lie at 2.7 - 3.2 au, with grain temperatures between 170
507
and 143 K, depending on the model (Hayashi 1981; Podolak &
508
Zucker 2004), In contrast, the current water snow line in the So-
509
lar System is estimated to be at ∼5 au from the Sun (Jewitt et al.
510
2007). Moreover, the condensation temperature of volatile com-
511
pounds is influenced by the properties of debris particles onto
512
which the gases freeze. Kim et al. (2019), for instance, found
513
that in the case of the young A-type star β Pic (HD 39060),
514
the water snow line could be located anywhere between 4.4 and
515
28.3 au, depending on the dust grain composition, grain size and
516
ice phase (amorphous or crystalline).
517
To explore the correlation between the locations of ice lines
518
and planetesimal belts within the subsample of debris disks spa-
519
tially resolved with SPHERE, we estimated the temperature of
520
their BB grains (Col. 5 in Table 2). These grains, being signifi-
521
cantly larger than the peak wavelength of the emitted disk spec-
522
trum, allow their temperature to be determined using the follow-
523
ing expression (Backman & Paresce 1993):
524
T bb = (278 K)
 L⋆
L⊙
!0.25  1 au
Rmes
belt
!0.5
,
525
where L⋆is the stellar luminosity, and Rmes
belt is the measured belt
526
radius in au (Col. 2 in Table 2).
527
According to this estimation, all resolved debris belts fall
528
into the category of exo-Kuiper belts containing cold dust
529
(Tbb < 100 K), with the exception of the warm dust belt around
530
HD 172555, which was detected with ZIMPOL (Engler et al.
531
2018). In Fig. 6, we present the derived BB temperatures of the
532
belts as a function of their measured radii. The shaded regions in
533
the plot indicate the upper temperature limits at which H2O, CO2
534
and CO may condense in young disks, depending on gas pressure
535
and dust temperature (Harsono et al. 2015). For comparison, the
536
plot also includes the locations of the Edgeworth–Kuiper belt at
537
40 au (Stern & Colwell 1997), with an estimated BB temperature
538
of Tbb = 44 K, and the main asteroid belt in the Solar System at
539
3.5 au (Wyatt 2008), with Tbb = 150 K.
540
As shown in Fig. 6, the majority of planetesimal belts are
541
located within the CO2 and CO ice formation zones. This trend
542
is also evident in seven double-belt systems, where both com-
543
ponents reside within the same ice-species region. Notably, all
544
three resolved planetesimal belts of HD 131835 lie within the
545
CO2 ice zone, suggesting that they originate from a common,
546
broad debris disk in which gaps may have been sculpted by plan-
547
etary bodies. The disk around HD 172555 is located in a region
548
where water molecules can accumulate on grain surfaces. This
549
analysis supports the hypothesis that planetesimals preferentially
550
form beyond the ice lines of various gas species.
551
If this statement holds true, the radial distance of a debris
552
belt should correlate with the luminosity of its host star. This re-
553
lationship has recently been examined in samples of debris disks
554
resolved at millimeter and far-IR wavelengths (Matrà et al. 2018;
555
Marshall et al. 2021). The subsample of debris disks spatially

belt ,
561
L⋆]. The scaling factor RL⊙is in au and represents the expected
562
radial position of a planetesimal belt around a star with solar
563
luminosity.
564
We obtained a relatively shallow linear dependence in log-
565
arithmic space log(Rbelt) = α log(L⋆/L⊙) + log(RL⊙) (magenta
566
dash-dotted line in Fig. 7) with RL⊙= 74 ± 7 au and α =
567
0.11 ± 0.05. These values remain within the 1σ uncertainties of
568
similar parameters reported in studies at millimeter and far-IR
569
wavelengths (Matrà et al. 2018; Marshall et al. 2021).
570
The HD 172555 disk was excluded from this analysis, as it is
571
the only system in our subsample that contains warm dust. How-

590
As expected, the power-law functions for both disk groups
591
are steeper than the function fitted to the entire sample. Notably,
592
the parameter RL⊙for the CO2 subsample is found to be 43 au.
593
This radial distance closely corresponds to the location of the
594
Edgeworth–Kuiper belt in the Solar System.
595
As previously discussed in this section, the radial locations
596
of the ice lines for volatile compounds vary over the course of
597
stellar evolution. To investigate whether a corresponding evolu-
598
tion in the radial positions of planetesimal belts is observable,
599
we plotted the measured belt radii as a function of stellar age for
600
the systems in the CO2 subsample. This subsample provides a
601
relatively larger number of systems with stars of similar spectral
602
type but different ages, allowing for a more meaningful compar-
603
ison.
604
Given that stellar luminosity is a key parameter in this con-
605
text, we examined three groups of stars categorized by spectral
606
type and mass: (1) A-type stars with 2.2 M⊙< M⋆< 2.6 M⊙,
607
(2) A-type stars with 1.9 M⊙< M⋆< 2.1 M⊙, and (3) F-type
608
stars with 1.2 M⊙< M⋆< 1.6 M⊙. For stars with multiple re-
609
solved belts, we adopted the mean belt radius, as all detected
610
belts in these systems have Tbb > 35 K.
611
We note, that the mean estimated age (Col. 11 in Table E.2)
612
of most stars with detected disks (90% of detections) is below
613
50 Myr. According to stellar evolutionary models (e.g., Palla &
614
Stahler 1999; Baraffe et al. 2015), such young objects are likely
615
located either on the pre-main-sequence (PMS) or on the zero-
616
age main sequence (ZAMS) in the Hertzsprung-Russell diagram.
617
Intermediate-mass stars (1 M⊙< M⋆< 3 M⊙) exhibit the
618
most pronounced luminosity evolution during their PMS phase.
619
These stars begin as fully convective objects with large radii
620
and high luminosities, which decrease as the stars contract. This
621
phase is followed by an increase in both temperature and lu-
622
minosity as a radiative core begins to develop because it be-
623
comes hotter and denser during the contraction phase. This pro-
624
cess leads to an increase in the rate of nuclear fusion, ultimately
625
stabilizing the star and placing it on the main sequence.
626
The luminosity evolution during the PMS phase depends
627
sensitively on the stellar mass and chemical composition (e.g.,
628
Tognelli et al. 2011). To illustrate this, Fig. 8 shows the evo-
629
Article number, page 11 of 57


## Page 12


A&A proofs: manuscript no. output
lution of the radial location of the CO2 freeze-out zones (red,
630
orange and blue shaded regions), corresponding to the CO2 zone
631
presented in Fig. 6, for stars with metallicity Z = 0.028 and
632
helium abundance Y = 0.304, calculated for stellar masses of
633
1.5 M⊙, 2 M⊙and 2.3 M⊙(Tognelli et al. 2011).
634
As shown in Fig. 8, stars in all three mass groups show a
635
trend of increasing belt radius with stellar age within the region
636
corresponding to the CO2 freeze-out zone. This behavior may re-
637
flect the outward migration of the CO2 ice line due to increasing
638
luminosity as the star evolves. To better quantify this result, we
639
performed a multiple regression analysis. We considered log tage
640
(in Myr) and log M⋆(in solar masses) as independent variables,
641
and log Rbelt (in au) as dependent variable. The best-fit relation
642
is the following:
643
log (Rbelt/au) = (0.37 ± 0.11) log (tage/Myr)
644
+ (0.59 ± 0.23) log (M/M⊙) + (1.27 ± 0.15).
645
646
Both coefficients are significant (with a 0.002 probability of be-
647
ing a chance result for the dependence on the age, and of 0.02
648
for the dependence on the mass). The dependence on age is then
649
highly significant. This relation predicts log Rbelt for stars in this
650
range of ages and masses with an accuracy of 0.12 dex.
651
4.4. Morphology of selected targets
652
In this section, we discuss debris systems that have been imaged
653
in scattered light for the first time, as well as debris disks whose
654
morphology exhibits notable features, such as multiple belts, that
655
warrant further examination.
656
HD 9672 (49 Ceti)
The A1V star HD 9672 is one of the
657
youngest and brightest stars in the sample. It is likely a mem-
658
ber of the ∼40 Myr old Argus MG (99% membership probabil-
659
ity; Zuckerman 2019). The debris disk surrounding HD 9672 is
660
gas-rich, with an estimated CO mass exceeding 2.2 × 10−4M⊕.
661
The spatial distribution of CO gas closely resembles the struc-
662
ture of the outer debris disk, whereas no molecular gas has been
663
detected within ∼90 au of the star (Hughes et al. 2008).
664
The thermal emission of the HD 9672 disk is well charac-
665
terized by two distinct dust populations: a warm component at
666
136−160 K and a cold component at 47−60 K (e.g., Wahhaj et al.
667
2007; Roberge et al. 2013; Chen et al. 2014, this work). Models
668
reproducing the disk’s emission at λ = 12.5 µm and λ = 17.9 µm
669
suggest that the warm dust grains are located within 60 au (Wah-
670
haj et al. 2007).
671
The debris disk has been observed multiple times with
672
SPHERE using different filters (e.g., Pawellek et al. 2019), in-
673
cluding the first detection of its scattered light (Choquet et al.
674
2017). The PCA-reduced data taken with the IRDIS B_Y filter,
675
shown in Fig. 9a, reveal a broad debris ring extending to the
676
image edges (∼6′′). This structure may consist of multiple nar-
677
row rings. In the r2−scaled image, the radial SB peak is located
678
between 144 and 156 au. Additionally, image residuals hint at
679
an inner planetesimal ring with a radius of ∼105-110 au (see
680
Fig. 9a). If this second cold debris ring is confirmed, it would
681
contain dust grains with a blackbody temperature of TBB = 54 K
682
and would not account for the warm dust excess observed in the
683
HD 9672 SED.
684
Polarized scattered light observations of the disk, conducted
685
with IRDIS in polarimetric mode using the B_Y filter, led to a
686
detection, albeit with a relatively low S/N (Fig. 5). This may be
687
attributed to an intrinsically low polarization fraction of the dust
688
in this disk.
689
HD 16743 The ALMA image of the debris disk around the F0
690
star HD 16743 was recently published by Marshall et al. (2023).
691
While the authors report only a marginal detection of the disk
692
in the IRDIS H-band data, our image, obtained with the IRDIS
693
H23 filter (Fig. 2), provides the first clear detection of this de-
694
bris disk in scattered light. This detection allows us to constrain
695
the disk’s geometrical parameters (Table 2). We determined that
696
the PA of the disk is ∼169◦, which closely matches the value re-
697
ported by Marshall et al. (2023). Additionally, our image reveals
698
an extended feature at a PA of approx. 17◦(see Fig. B.2). The
699
origin of this feature remains unclear; it is most likely a residual
700
PSF artifact, possibly caused by the telescope spider. However,
701
the possibility of a scattered light signal cannot be entirely ruled
702
out.
703
HD 36546
HD 36546 is another A-type star in our sample
704
(A0V-A2V; Lisse et al. 2017; Currie et al. 2017), located at a
705
distance of 100.1 pc. It is a probable member of the Mamajek
706
17 group, with an estimated age between 3 and 10 Myr. For the
707
first time, its debris disk has been spatially resolved using the
708
Subaru/HiCIAO camera in the H band (Currie et al. 2017).
709
Our observations reveal a well-defined debris ring in the IFS
710
data at a radial separation of 55±10 au, as shown in Fig. 9c. This
711
ring is also visible in the IRDIS image in the K band (Fig. 9b)
712
albeit with a lower S/N. Additionally, the IRDIS image, along
713
with some IFS data, reveals a more extended debris belt with a
714
radius of 110 ± 30 au (outer ring in Fig. 9b). This belt appears
715
both wider and brighter than the inner ring and may consist of
716
multiple components. The presence of two cold debris rings at
717
approx. 55 and 110 au aligns with the modeling results of Cur-
718
rie et al. (2017) and Lawson et al. (2021), who determined that
719
the debris disk of HD 36546 extends between 60 and 115 au.
720
However, due to the system’s relatively high inclination (∼80◦),
721
precisely determining the number of rings present remains chal-
722
lenging.
723
The residual pattern observed within the inner ring (Fig. 9b)
724
resembles the structure of a smaller, distinct ring, particularly
725
in its alignment along the major axis of the outer rings. If this
726
feature represents a genuine ring rather than PSF residuals, an
727
alternative that cannot be ruled out, its radial separation from the
728
star would be ∼30 au. Interestingly, Lisse et al. (2017) previ-
729
ously reported a debris belt at ∼135 K, which is expected to be
730
located between 20 and 40 au from HD 36546. Moreover, when
731
fitting the disk spectrum and photometric data from multiple in-
732
struments, Lisse et al. (2017) also predicted the existence of an
733
additional inner belt with a temperature of ∼570 K, correspond-
734
ing to hotter dust located between 1.2 and 2.2 au within the sys-
735
tem.
736
HD 36968 HD 36968 is a young (∼20 Myr) F2V star located at
737
149 pc in Octans Association (Moór et al. 2011; Murphy & Law-
738
son 2014). The debris disk surrounding this star exhibits a high
739
IR excess of 1.34 × 10−3 and has been detected for the first time
740
in both total scattered intensity and polarized intensity (Figs. 2
741
and 5). The fundamental geometrical parameters of the disk are
742
provided in Table 2.
743
HD 92945 HD 92945 is a nearby K1V star located at a distance
744
of 21.51 pc from the Sun. We resolved the inner debris belt with
745
a radius of ∼56 au and a part of the outer belt at ∼119 au (Fig. 2),
746
both of which were previously imaged with ALMA (Marino
747
et al. 2019). The PCA reduction of the H-band data shows resid-
748
ual structures that suggest the presence of a third dust ring with
749
a possible radius of ∼38 au.

HD 98800 HD 98800 is an intriguing quadruple system located
751
42.1 pc from the Sun (Gaia Collaboration et al. 2021; Boden
752
et al. 2005). As a member of the TW Hydrae Association, it has
753
an estimated age between 7 and 10 Myr (Ducourant et al. 2014).
754
The system consists of two pairs of spectroscopic binaries which

757
A double-lined SB BaBb (MBa = 0.70 M⊙, MBb = 0.58 M⊙,
758
P = 315 days; Boden et al. 2005) is surrounded by a bright tran-
759
sitional debris disk, previously imaged at 1.3 mm with ALMA
760
(Kennedy et al. 2019) and at 8.8 mm and 5 cm with the Very
761
Large Array (VLA) (Ribas et al. 2018).
762
Imaging this disk in scattered light presents a significant
763
challenge due to its small angular size and the presence of a
764
close central binary. The disk has a radius of less than 0.1′′
765
Article number, page 13 of 57


## Page 14


A&A proofs: manuscript no. output
and an inclination of less than 45◦, making non-coronagraphic
766
polarimetric differential imaging (PDI) with ZIMPOL the most
767
suitable observing strategy for resolving it in scattered polar-
768
ized light. Such observations were conducted on April 14, 2016
769
(ESO 097.C-0344, PI: Kennedy), and our data reduction suc-
770
cessfully detected scattered light from the disk. Figure 5 presents
771
the Qϕ image obtained using the ZIMPOL R_PRIME filter.
772
The HD 98800 transitional disk is the smallest detected with
773
SPHERE/ZIMPOL in both angular and physical size (Rbelt ≈
774
0.07′′ or 3 au). In Fig. 5, the white bar in the HD 98800 panel
775
showing the Qϕ image represents 0.5′′ (20 au), whereas in all
776
other panels it corresponds to 1′′.
777
The Stokes Q and U signals, used to compute the Qϕ image,
778
are partially reduced due to the significant PSF convolution ef-
779
fect caused by the small angular size of the disk (Engler et al.
780
2018). Additionally, the disk signal may be affected by resid-
781
ual flux from the central binary, as complete removal of stellar
782
flux in the center of image is not possible even with PDI. These
783
stellar residuals are always present at the image center and origi-
784
nate either from nonzero polarization of the star(s) or from slight
785
mismatches in the PSF shapes of the two orthogonal polarization
786
states, which do not perfectly align. These mismatches arise due
787
to short coherence times and, particularly relevant for a binary
788
system, differences between the PSF of a binary star and that of
789
a single point source. For the HD 98800 disk, we estimated the
790
extent of the stellar residuals from the BaBb binary in the Qϕ im-
791
age by analyzing the residuals from the AaAb binary, which is
792
located at ∼0.7′′ from BaBb, just outside of the frame in Fig. 5.
793
These stellar residuals have been masked in the central region of
794
the presented image.
795
The scattered polarized light from the disk is detected be-
796
tween 0.06′′ (2.52 au) and 0.12′′ (5.06 au). Two dips in SB are
797
visible on the eastern (PA = 109◦) and western (PA = 280◦)
798
sides of the disk, which may be attributed to stellar PSF ef-
799
fects. Based on the SB distribution in the Qϕ image, we de-
800
rived the geometrical parameters of the disk, as listed in Table 2.
801
Within uncertainties, these parameters are in good agreement
802
with those obtained from VLA and ALMA images (Ribas et al.
803
2018; Kennedy et al. 2019).
804
HD 111520
The strong brightness asymmetry in the scattered
805
light of the nearly edge-on disk around HD 111520 (F5/6V star
806
at d = 108 pc) has been previously observed with HST (Pad-
807
gett & Stapelfeldt 2016) and GPI (Draper et al. 2016a). In the
808
SPHERE/IFS image, the northern extension of the debris disk
809
appears significantly brighter than the southern extension, where
810
a dip in SB is observed at approx. 0.5′′. Within 0.8′′, the disk
811
morphology resembles that of AU Mic disk. The SB variations
812
along the major axis in both systems may be explained by the
813
presence of a spiral disk structure or a set of non-coplanar debris
814
rings. Indeed, the SED of HD 111520 is best fitted with multi-
815
ple dust populations at different temperatures, suggesting the ex-
816
istence of radially separated debris belts containing both warm
817
and cold dust.
818
HD 120326 The two distinct cold dust belts around the F0V star
819
HD 120326 were first resolved in scattered light with SPHERE
820
(Bonnefoy et al. 2017). We measure a radial distance of ∼119 au
821
(1.05′′) for the larger planetesimal belt and ∼50 au (0.44′′) for
822
the smaller one. The polarimetric data of HD 120326 reveal po-
823
larized light between 0.25′′ and 0.7′′ with a tentative SB peak at
824
∼0.5′′, potentially indicating the presence of an additional inner
825
debris belt in this system.
826
HD 129590
HD 129590 is a G3V star with one of the high-
827
est IR excesses in our sample (fdisk = (6.3 ± 1.8) × 10−3). The
828
star is surrounded by two planetesimal belts, forming a struc-
829
ture reminiscent of a “moth” shape (Matthews et al. 2017; Olof-
830
sson et al. 2023) similar to that observable in the HD 61005 disk
831
(Buenzli et al. 2010). The inner belt, located at ∼49 au, is bright
832
and exhibits an extended halo of small dust particles. In con-
833
trast, the outer planetesimal ring is significantly fainter but re-
834
mains clearly visible in the PCA-reduced total intensity images
835
(Fig. 9d). The region between the two belts does not appear to
836
be completely cleared. The polarized intensity data show that
837
although the outer ring is less pronounced in the halo, it re-
838
mains detectable (Fig. 9e). This ring likely extends between 80
839
and 92 au, with a peak SB measured at ∼82 au in the r2-scaled
840
polarized intensity image. Recently, CO gas was detected in the
841
system (Kral et al. 2020), supporting the possibility of gas pileup
842
as a contributing factor to the observed disk structure (Olofsson
843
et al. 2023).
844
HD 145560
We resolve the debris disk around HD 145560
845
(F5V star at 121.23 pc) in total intensity using the RDI tech-
846
nique (Xie et al. 2022) applied to the H2H3 dataset taken with
847
IRDIS (Fig. 2), as well as in polarized intensity using the VBB
848
filter of ZIMPOL (Fig. 9h). This disk has also been observed
849
with ALMA (Lieman-Sifry et al. 2016; Matrà et al. 2025) and
850
GPI (Esposito et al. 2020). Among all available data for this tar-
851
get, the ZIMPOL image provides the highest spatial resolution
852
and appears to reveal a spiral-like structure on the southern side
853
of the disk, as well as a point-source-like residual (denoted as
854
“PS?” in Fig. 9h) on the western side. However, this image is
855
affected by low-wind effects and a short coherence time during
856
the observation, which lowered the S/N of the polarimetric data,
857
making the detection of this structure uncertain. The apparent
858
point source could, in reality, be a bright part of the disk.
859
The scattered light in both SPHERE images exhibits an el-
860
liptical structure, with a major axis PA = 39 ± 5.0◦and a radius
861
of r = 87 ± 5 au, as measured from the r2-scaled image, and
862
an inclination of 48.7 ± 7.0◦. These geometrical parameters are
863
within 1σ in good agreement with the GPI measurement (Espos-
864
ito et al. 2020). However, there is a noticeable offset when com-
865
paring the disk’s orientation on the sky as measured with ALMA:
866
20 ± 7.0◦at 1.24 mm (Lieman-Sifry et al. 2016) and 28 ± 8.0◦at
867
1.3 mm (Matrà et al. 2025). This discrepancy may be due to the
868
different spatial and angular resolutions between the scattered
869
light images (SPHERE, GPI) and the thermal emission images
870
(ALMA). Since the HD 145560 disk is the only resolved disk in
871
our sample that shows such a PA deviation compared to ALMA
872
data, this offset may suggest a more complex disk structure than
873
a simple ring. Possible explanations include a spiral structure or
874
the presence of multiple planetesimal rings at different PAs and
875
inclinations.

HD 157587 The debris disk around F5V star HD 157587 is re-
877
solved with SPHERE instruments in both total and polarized

H (Fig. 9f), the residual pattern inside the disk resembles the

ities (e.g., Hughes et al. 2011; Faramaz et al. 2021, and refer-
896
ences therein). Within the large disk cavity (r ∼100 au), four
897
giant planets with masses below 10 MJup have been discovered
898
(Marois et al. 2008, 2010) and extensively studied (e.g., Esposito
899
et al. 2013; Zurlo et al. 2016; Wang et al. 2018, see also Sect. 7).
900
The relatively low inclination (∼30◦) of the debris belt facilitates
901
planet detection but makes the belt itself challenging to observe
902
using DI with the ADI technique.
903
HD 218396 was observed in various modes with all SPHERE
904
instruments. In the imaging modes, the cold debris belt, extend-
905
ing between ∼80 and 350 au (2′′ −7.5′′) and peaking in SB at
906
180 −200 au (∼4.5′′, Faramaz et al. 2021), was not detected.
907
Similarly, in the H-band polarized intensity image, the disk re-
908
mains either undetectable or barely visible, likely due to its low
909
SB in polarized light. However, the image reveals a bright ring-
910
like structure near the coronagraph, at a radial distance of ∼0.4′′
911
or ∼15 au (Fig. 9i).
912
The possibility that this structure results from stellar PSF
913
residuals cannot be entirely excluded. HD 218396 was observed
914
with IRDIS in polarimetric mode on two nights: October 11 and
915
October 13, 2016. The ring-like structure is detected in the data
916
from October 11, when the observing conditions were signifi-
917
cantly better (seeing between 0.44′′ and 0.78′′, and coherence
918
times between 3.5 and 6.1 ms) compared to those on October 13
919
(seeing between 0.92′′ and 2.82′′, and coherence times between
920
1.7 and 4.0 ms). Poor observing conditions, such as those during
921
the second night, can completely prevent the detection of a de-
922
bris disk (see discussion in Sect. 6). Therefore, the non-detection
923
of the ring in the data from the second night does not rule out the
924
presence of a warm planetesimal belt at the considered radial
925
position. Additionally, PSF residuals of this kind, especially at
926
locations farther from the coronagraph and AO ring, are uncom-
927
mon in IRDIS polarimetric data. Thus, the imaged ring likely
928
traces polarized scattered light from dust particles in a second,
929
inner debris belt.
930
The idea that this feature originates from a warm dust belt
931
is supported by flux measurements of HD 218396 obtained with
932
IRAS, ISO, and Spitzer space telescopes. Based on these data,
933
Su et al. (2009) modeled the disk SED with three distinct dust
934
components: a warm belt, a cold belt, and an extended halo.
935
Stronger evidence for the presence of a warm belt at ∼15 au
936
comes from recent JWST/MIRI observations of HD 218396 at
937
mid-IR wavelengths (Boccaletti et al. 2024) which provided spa-
938
tially resolved signatures of the inner disk component.
939
If these interpretations are correct, the IRDIS H-band image
940
resolves the inner warm dust belt in scattered polarized light for
941
the first time. This belt has a radial distance of r = 15.5 ± 1.8 au,
942
an inclination of i = (32±7)◦, and a PA of (39±22)◦, and it may
943
have an offset from the central star.
944
TWA 7 TWA 7 is a 4.4 ± 1.4 Myr old (Herczeg & Hillenbrand
945
2014) M2Ve star in the TW Hydrae association. The IRDIS po-
946
larimetric H-band image (Fig. 9g) reveals a nearly pole-on sys-
947
tem consisting of three rings at approx. 27, 52 and 93 au (Olofs-
948
son et al. 2018; Ren et al. 2021). The disk in the region between
949
the inner and middle rings exhibits a clumpy structure, with arc-
950
like streamers particularly evident in the area extending from the
951
middle ring to the outer boundary of the detected scattered-light
952
emission at ∼96 au. These structural features are most clearly
953
visible on the southern side of the disk, which is inclined toward
954
the observer and exhibits enhanced SB due to the forward scat-
955
tering of stellar light by dust grains. In the Qϕ image (Fig. 9g),
956
we highlight three such features, labeled “F1”, “F2”, and “F3”,
957
all of which have been detected in at least three separate epochs
958
of IRDIS polarimetric observations, albeit with varying S/N (see
959
also Sect. B and Fig. B.1). The most pronounced of these, “F2”,
960
was also identified in HST/STIS and HST/NICMOS data (Ren
961
et al. 2021).
962
These arc-like features may share a similar origin with the
963
fast-moving clumps observed in the edge-on disk of AU Mic
964
(Boccaletti et al. 2018). In both systems, sub-micron dust grains
965
may be expelled by strong stellar winds from their active M-
966
type host stars (Strubbe & Chiang 2006; Schüppler et al. 2015),
967
potentially triggered by collisions in a secondary belt or in the
968
vicinity of a planetary companion (e.g., Chiang & Fung 2017;
969
Sezestre et al. 2017). Notably, a candidate Saturn-mass planet
970
located at a projected distance of ∼52 au or 1.5′′, coincident
971
with the position of TWA 7’s second planetesimal ring, has re-
972
cently been detected with JWST/MIRI (Lagrange et al. 2025).
973
This ring is both very narrow and flanked by two gaps, appearing
974
underluminous at the planet’s location relative to other azimuths
975
(Fig. 9g). Such a morphology supports the scenario of a resonant
976
planetesimal ring sculpted by the planet, which may be carving
977
the adjacent gaps and generating a local void.
978
If confirmed, this planetary companion could be responsible
979
for gravitational perturbations that locally enhance dust produc-
980
tion. Once released, small grains are redistributed by interactions
981
with stellar wind and radiation pressure, giving rise to asymmet-
982
ric structures such as arcs, streamers, or clumps, depending on
983
the disk inclination and viewing geometry. The nearly pole-on
984
orientation of the TWA 7 disk may thus offer a complementary
985
view of the dynamic processes that shape AU Mic’s edge-on
986
disk.
987
BD-20 951
The highly inclined circumbinary debris disk
988
around the SB2 BD-20 951 (Torres et al. 2008) is resolved for
989
the first time in both total and polarized scattered light with
990
SPHERE in the H band (Perrot et al. in prep). The primary is
991
a K1V(e) star, and the binary components have an estimated flux
992
ratio of ∼0.25 (Elliott et al. 2014). The system may be a member
993
of the Carina MG (28±11 Myr; Gratton et al. 2024) or Tucana-
994
Horologium association (37±11 Myr; Gratton et al. 2024) as pro-
995
posed by Torres et al. (2008), although the BANYAN Σ tool clas-
996
sifies it as a field star.
997
The IR excess was identified by Moór et al. (2016) who noted
998
that the colder component may significantly contribute to the to-
999
tal near-IR flux of the system. The residuals in the PCA-reduced 1000
image suggest that the disk possesses sweap-back wings (Fig. 2). 1001
The geometrical parameters of belt are specified in Table 2.
1002
4.5. Modeling of selected planetesimal belts
1003
To investigate the morphology of the detected debris disks and 1004
potential correlations between disk parameters and host star 1005
properties, we fitted images of several debris belts (listed in Ta- 1006
ble E.1) using a grid of models that simulate single scattering of 1007
stellar photons by dust particles in an optically thin disk. A key 1008
advantage of our approach, compared to studies focused on indi- 1009
vidual disks, is the use of a uniform modeling framework for all 1010
systems in our sample. This consistency allows for a more direct 1011
and meaningful comparison of the derived disk parameters.
1012
4.5.1. Model for scattered light
1013
To estimate the fundamental geometric parameters of the debris 1014
belts, we generated synthetic images of scattered (or polarized) 1015
light and compared them with the observed disk images. To cre- 1016
ate these synthetic images, we employed a 3D, rotationally sym- 1017
metric model to describe the spatial distribution of grain num- 1018
ber density, ngr(r, h), within the disk. Following the approach of 1019
Augereau et al. (1999), we characterize this distribution as the 1020
product of a radial profile R(r) and a Gaussian function Z(r, h). 1021
The profile R(r) defines the variation of grain number density in 1022
the disk midplane as a function of radial distance from the star r. 1023

1024
of ngr(r, h), shaping its distribution in the direction perpendicular
1025
to the disk midplane, as described by the height coordinate h:
1026

1027
where r0 is the reference radius of the debris belt, αin > 0 and
1028
αout < 0 are the exponents of the radial power laws for the
1029
dust distributions inside and outside of the belt, respectively. The
1030
scale height of the disk H(r) is defined as a half-width at half-
1031
maximum (HWHM) of the Gaussian profile at radial distance r.
1032
In this work, the scale height is assumed to increase linearly with
1033
radial distance, following the relation H(r) = H0 (r/r0)β, where
1034
H0 = H(r0) and the disk flaring index β is fixed at β = 1.
1035
In the model, each dust grain location within the disk is as-
1036
sociated with a scattering angle θ, defined as the angle between
1037
the incident stellar ray striking a dust particle and the observer’s
1038
line of sight, where θ = 0 corresponds to forward scattering.
1039
The scattering angle is a crucial model parameter, as it gov-
1040
erns the fraction of incident stellar light scattered in a particu-
1041
lar direction, described by the so-called scattering phase func-
1042
tion (SPF). To generate synthetic disk images, we employed
1043
the Henyey-Greenstein (HG) function as the SPF (Henyey &
1044
Greenstein 1941), which provides a convenient parametrization
1045
of anisotropic scattering by dust grains:
1046
S PF(θ, g) =
1 −g2
4π(1 + g2 −2 g cos θ)3/2 ,
1047
where g represents the HG scattering asymmetry parameter.
1048
This parameter quantifies the preferential direction of scattering,
1049
ranging from g = −1 (backward scattering) to g = 1 (forward
1050
scattering), with g = 0 corresponding to isotropic scattering.
1051
Based on observations of scattered light from zodiacal and
1052
cometary dust in the Solar System (e.g., Leinert et al. 1976;
1053
Bertini et al. 2017), as well as laboratory experiments with dust
1054
analogs (e.g., Frattin et al. 2019; Muñoz et al. 2017), interplane-
1055
tary dust grains are expected to preferentially scatter radiation in
1056
the forward direction. As a result, in disk images, the side of the
1057
disk that is closer to the observer appears brighter.
1058
The forward-scattering behavior of dust particles can be de-
1059
scribed using a HG function with a positive asymmetry parame-
1060
ter (g > 0). However, real SPFs derived from observational data
1061
often exhibit a more complex structure: a pronounced diffrac-
1062
tion peak at small scattering angles (g >> 0), a relatively flat
1063
mid-range (g ∼0), and an enhanced backscattering component
1064
(g < 0). Consequently, a more accurate representation of an ac-
1065
tual SPF would require a combination of three HG functions.
1066
Nevertheless, we opted to model the data using a single HG
1067
function. This choice is justified by the fact that, in most cases,
1068
the inclination of resolved debris disks does not permit the mea-
1069
surement of scattering intensity across the full range of scatter-
1070
ing angles (0◦to 180◦). Instead, we can only fit the portion of
1071
the SPF that is accessible in the data, which can be adequately
1072
approximated by a single HG function.
1073
Another simplification adopted in our modeling approach is
1074
the assumption that the optical characteristics of dust grains, de-
1075
fined by their composition, shape, and size, are spatially uniform
1076
across the disk and can be represented by a single SPF. While
1077
this assumption simplifies the modeling process, it remains a
1078
coarse approximation, as the SPF is inherently dependent on dust
1079
properties that are expected to vary with radial distance. For in-
1080
stance, at the radial location of the peak grain number density,
1081
Rbelt, debris spanning a wide range of sizes is typically present,
1082
from submicron grains to kilometer-sized planetesimals (e.g.,
1083
Wyatt 2008). In contrast, the outer disk is expected to form a
1084
halo of small grains that are collisionally produced within the
1085
main ring and subsequently placed on high-eccentricity orbits 1086
by stellar radiation pressure. This halo is anticipated to exhibit 1087
strong size segregation (Thebault et al. 2014), with the dominant 1088
grain size decreasing with increasing radial distance.
1089
Depending on the quality of the available data, we fitted ei- 1090
ther an image of total intensity or polarized intensity. To generate 1091
a polarized intensity image (Qϕ image), we employed a polar- 1092
ized scattering phase function (pSPF) given by a functional form 1093
(e.g., Engler et al. 2017)
1094
pS PF(θ, g) = pmax

1096
In this equation, we employed a polarization fraction func- 1097
tion p(θ) characteristic of Rayleigh scattering, in which the max- 1098
imum polarization fraction is attained at a scattering angle of 1099
θ = 90◦(Bohren & Huffman 1983):
1100

(3)
Rayleigh scattering describes the scattering of light by par- 1101
ticles whose sizes are at least an order of magnitude smaller 1102
than the wavelength of the incident radiation. In near-IR obser- 1103
vations of debris disks, this condition is generally satisfied in the 1104
outer disk regions, where the abundance of larger grains declines 1105
and the contribution of small particles to the scattered light be- 1106
comes increasingly significant. The polarization fraction func- 1107
tion of this small-particle population as well as that of micron- 1108
sized grains, typically traced in near-IR scattered-light images, 1109
can be reasonably approximated by the Rayleigh scattering func- 1110
tion p(θ), as defined in Eq. 3 (see Appendix C).
1111
For each disk specified in Table E.1, we generated a large 1112
set of models using a grid of fitting parameters. The parameter 1113
ranges were individually defined based on the findings of pre- 1114
vious studies on these targets. We deliberately chose to use a 1115
model grid approach rather than a Markov Chain Monte Carlo 1116
(MCMC) algorithm, which, although widely employed in disk 1117
modeling, often yields unrealistically small uncertainties on the 1118
best-fitting parameters. Mazoyer et al. (2020) showed indeed that 1119
MCMC often leads to underestimated uncertainties, an effect 1120
probably due to the non-Gaussian statistics of the residual noise 1121
in coronagraphic images (Pairet et al. 2019).
1122
To identify a family of models that adequately reproduce the 1123
observed disk images, we therefore adopted a more conservative 1124
threshold for the χ2 value than the one derived from the prob- 1125
ability distribution of parameters, which assumes normally dis- 1126
tributed errors:
1127
χ2 < χ2
min + ∆χ2,
1128
where χ2
min is the minimum chi-square value obtained from the 1129
fits, and ∆χ2 =
√
2ν with ν denoting the number of degrees of 1130
freedom8 (Thalmann et al. 2013). The mean values and standard 1131
deviations of the parameter distributions from the family of well- 1132
fitting models are adopted as the best-fitting parameters and their 1133
corresponding uncertainties, as reported in Table E.1. Note that 1134
these estimates can be affected by the parameter step size, as it 1135
determines the sample size and can influence the precision of the 1136
evaluated sample mean and standard deviation9. Also, the best- 1137
fitting parameters do not correspond to the single model with the 1138
minimum χ2, but rather reflect the statistical properties of the 1139
family of acceptable models.

For the HD 39060 (β Pic) debris disk, the model
1141
consists of outer and inner planetesimal belts and has, therefore,

1150
Ahmic et al. 2009).
1151
In the polarized intensity image of HD 39060 (Qϕ image), the

1153

1154

1155

1156

1164
from the inner belt relative to the vertical axis of the image.
1165
HD 39060 is the only target for which we applied a double-

belt model. Other targets, such as HD 15115 (Engler et al. 2019;
