sharp inner edges, a feature often interpreted as evidence of un- 1246
seen planets clearing the space at the edges of the planetesimal 1247
belts. Milli et al. (2017b) tested methods to constrain large values 1248
of the αin and αout parameters and concluded that the modeling 1249
of these parameters is limited by the intrinsic steepness of the 1250
PSF. Specifically, belt edges that are steeper than the PSF wings 1251
are inherently blurred by convolution with the PSF, making it 1252
impossible to constrain αin and αout values steeper than approxi- 1253
mately 30 in the IRDIS H band.
1254
If the aforementioned targets are excluded, a trend emerges 1255
in which the αin parameter increases with stellar luminosity 1256
(Fig. 11c). Regarding αout (Fig. 11f), the derived values for most 1257
systems are consistent with the archival data presented in Ta- 1258
Article number, page 18 of 57


## Page 19


Engler et al.: Debris Disks demographics
ble 1 of Thebault et al. (2023). As discussed in that study, the
1259
expected αout value for a typical belt-like system with an outer
1260
halo composed of small grains placed on high-eccentricity or-
1261
bits by radiation pressure is approx. −2.5. When considering er-
1262
ror bars, we find that roughly half of the systems in Table E.1
1263
have αout estimates that are compatible with this reference value.
1264
For the remaining systems, we obtain steeper outer profiles, with
1265
αout reaching values as low as -8 or even -12. In these cases, ad-
1266
ditional dynamical processes, such as perturbations by external
1267
planets or stellar companions, are likely responsible for clearing
1268
out the outer disk regions (Thebault et al. 2023).
1269
Vertical disk structure
1270
As discussed in Sect. 4.5.1, we adopted a Gaussian function
1271
to describe the vertical profile of each disk, accounting for its
1272
nonzero vertical width. In this study, we define the HWHM of
1273
the Gaussian profile as the scale height H(r) of the disk at a
1274
given radial distance r. The ratio of the scale height to the radial
1275
distance, Adisk = H(r)/r, is referred to as the disk aspect ratio,
1276
also known as the disk half-opening angle. Since, in our model,
1277
the scale height H(r) varies linearly with distance r, the aspect
1278
ratio remains constant throughout the disk and can be expressed
1279
as Adisk = H0/r0, where H0 is the scale height at the reference
1280
radius r0.
1281
The scale height and aspect ratio of a disk serve as key indi-
1282
cators of the dynamical excitation within a debris system. The-
1283
bault (2009) numerically estimated a minimum aspect ratio of
1284
0.04±0.02 for a collisionally evolving disk observed at visible to
1285
mid-IR wavelengths. If the disk experiences additional dynami-
1286
cal perturbations from massive bodies such as giant exoplanets,
1287
its aspect ratio is expected to exceed 0.06.
1288
In Figures 11d and 11e, we show the results of our anal-
1289
ysis of the aspect ratios obtained for the modeled disks. We
1290
consider the disk to be resolved in direction perpendicular to
1291
the disk midplane, if the fitted FWHM of the vertical profile
1292
is larger than the FWHM of the stellar PSF. For four mod-
1293
eled disks (HD 114082, HD 117214, HD 120326, HD 131488)
1294
which are shown in Fig. 11d with open markers this condi-
1295
tion is not fulfilled. We note that the parameters of these four
1296
disks are derived from the fitting of the total intensity images
1297
applying ADI forward-modeling approach. Therefore the value
1298
of the scale height might be underestimated. The other two disks
1299
(HD 109573, HD 181327) are only marginally resolved in verti-
1300
cal direction. Both disks have an inclination lower than 80◦. In
1301
particular the HD 181327 disk has an inclination of ∼30◦which
1302
is the lowest one we modeled. This might reflect the challenge
1303
of modeling the disk scale height when using images obtained
1304
with the ADI technique or images of low inclined disks.
1305
Most of the modeled disks have a scale height between 0.02
1306
and 0.06 (Fig. 11d). This relatively small vertical extent can be
1307
explained by the combined effects of radiation pressure acting
1308
on small dust particles and their mutual collisions, which natu-
1309
rally regulate the disk’s thickness (Thebault 2009). In contrast,
1310
the HD 172555 disk exhibits a significant larger scale height of
1311
0.1, which may indicate the influence of additional massive per-
1312
turbers, although this result could be affected by the lower S/N
1313
of the image. This system is particularly intriguing, as it contains
1314
detected gas and a notable abundance of hot, small dust grains,
1315
features that may be the aftermath of a recent, violent collision
1316
between planetary bodies (Lisse et al. 2017; Riviere-Marichalar
1317
et al. 2012; Kiefer et al. 2014; Engler et al. 2018; Schneiderman
1318
et al. 2021; Samland et al. 2025).
1319
Three targets (HD 106906, HD 115600, and HD 129590)
1320
with modeled scale heights ranging between 0.06 and 0.07 are
1321
enclosed by the red ellipse in Fig. 11d. All three disks are highly
1322
inclined and are suspected to host at least two cold belts. If so,
1323
one possible explanation for the relatively large scale heights in-
1324
ferred from the models is that the inner belts remain unresolved
1325
but lie in close projected proximity to the outer belts along
1326
the minor axis in the scattered-light images. This configuration 1327
could mimic the appearance of a geometrically thicker planetes- 1328
imal belt. An alternative explanation, at least for HD 106906, is 1329
dynamical excitation of the disk by a massive substellar com- 1330
panion known to be present in the system (see Sect. 7). It is also 1331
worth noting that the HD 129590 disk contains small amounts of 1332
CO gas (MCO = 10−5 −10−4M⊕; Kral et al. 2020). In contrast, 1333
no gas has been detected so far in HD 106906 and HD 115600, 1334
leaving the influence of gas on the observed disk scale heights in 1335
these systems uncertain.
1336
However, there are four gas-rich systems with the estimated 1337
CO masses exceeding 10−2 M⊕, namely HD 9672, HD 32297, 1338
HD 121617 (Moór et al. 2019), and HD 131488 (Moór et al. 1339
2017; Pawellek et al. 2024). These systems are marked by trian- 1340
gles in Figs. 11d and 11e, and their aspect ratios span a range be- 1341
tween 0.026 and 0.011. In these disks, the CO emission has been 1342
observed to be axisymmetric and co-located with the millimeter- 1343
sized dust particles (Hughes et al. 2017; Moór et al. 2017; Mac- 1344
Gregor et al. 2018). The relatively low aspect ratios derived for 1345
these gas-rich systems may suggest that micron-sized dust grains 1346
are dynamically coupled to the gas, leading to their settling to- 1347
ward the disk midplane.
1348
There appears to be a trend of decreasing disk aspect ra- 1349
tio with increasing stellar luminosity, as observed in Fig. 11e. 1350
This trend could also account for the low aspect ratios of gas- 1351
rich disks, as three of these systems are associated with high- 1352
luminosity A-type stars (L⋆> 13 L⊙), and, especially as we de- 1353
rive an aspect ratio of Adisk = 0.017 for the debris belt around the 1354
AOV star HD 109573 (L⋆= 25.2 L⊙), which is not known to be 1355
gas-rich, further supporting this possible correlation. However, 1356
confirming this trend would require a significantly larger sample 1357
of measured aspect ratios.
1358
5. SED modeling
1359
We applied SED modeling to characterize the thermal emission 1360
of debris disks in our sample, using two different approaches 1361
to fit the photometric data. The modified BB (MBB) approach 1362
(Backman & Paresce 1993) provides a uniform fitting method 1363
for all targets, making it particularly suitable for a statistical 1364
analysis of the sample. In contrast, the particle size distribution 1365
(SD) approach (e.g., Müller et al. 2010; Pawellek et al. 2021) 1366
allows for a more detailed characterization of dust grain proper- 1367
ties, including the dominant grain size, the SD steepness, and the 1368
bulk optical properties of the dust. The SD model is only appli- 1369
cable to spatially resolved debris disks, as determining the disk 1370
radius is necessary to break the degeneracy between the location 1371
of dust particles and their sizes (Pawellek et al. 2014).
1372
5.1. Modeling procedure
1373
We utilized photometric data for our sample from published cat- 1374
alogs, such as 2MASS (Cutri et al. 2003), the WISE All-Sky Re- 1375
lease Catalog (Wright et al. 2010), the AKARI All-Sky Catalog 1376
(Ishihara et al. 2010), the Spitzer Heritage Archive (Carpenter 1377
et al. 2008; Lebouteiller et al. 2011; Chen et al. 2014; Sierchio 1378
et al. 2014), and the Herschel Point Source Catalog (Marton et al. 1379
2015; Marshall et al. 2021). In addition, we used data published 1380
in the literature (e.g., Chen et al. 2014; Matrà et al. 2017; Mar- 1381
shall et al. 2021). These data allow us to analyze the SEDs and 1382
assess the presence of IR excess emission beyond what is ex- 1383
pected from the stellar photosphere.
1384
To find excess emission, we fitted an SED model consisting 1385
of a star and a disk. Firstly, we fitted PHOENIX stellar photo- 1386
sphere models (Brott & Hauschildt 2005) for each target using 1387
the stellar luminosity and the stellar temperature as model pa- 1388
rameters, and photometric data in the VIS/NIR where the stellar 1389
emission is supposed to dominate the SED and the disk emission 1390
is negligible. The resulting stellar luminosities and temperatures 1391
Article number, page 19 of 57


## Page 20


A&A proofs: manuscript no. output
are listed in Table E.2. Secondly, knowing the stellar contribu-
1392
tion to the mid- and far-IR data, we derived the excess emission
1393
in the appropriate wavelength bands between ∼5 and ∼1000 µm
1394
taking into account the uncertainties of the photometry and the
1395
photospheric model.
1396
We followed the four criteria given in Ballering et al. (2013)
1397
and Pawellek et al. (2014) to check for the presence of a warm
1398
disk component in addition to the cold Kuiper belt analog.
1399
Firstly, the number of photometric data points must be large
1400
enough so that the data are not over-fitted. If that was the case in
1401
a second step we considered a warm component to be present,
1402
if there is a significant excess (≥3σ) in either the WISE/22
1403
or MIPS/24 in excess of that which could originate in a single
1404
ring fitted to longer wavelength data. Thirdly, the fit of the two-
1405
component SED has to be much better than the one-component
1406
fit according to the Bayesian information criterion (BIC)
1407
BIC = χ2 + J loge (Ndata),
(5)
where J represents the number of free parameters and Ndata the
1408
number of data points. We used the classification given in Kass
1409
& Raftery (1995) to infer whether a one- or a two-component
1410
model is more likely (Pawellek et al. 2021). As a fourth crite-
1411
rion, we required the inferred ring containing the warm dust to
1412
be located outside the sublimation radius (assuming 1300 K as
1413
the sublimation temperature for astrosilicate). If all four criteria
1414
were met, we assumed the SED to consist of a two-component
1415
model.
1416
The uncertainties of the fit parameters were inferred in the
1417
following way. We started at the position of the minimum χ2 in
1418
parameter space, e.g., from the best-fitting fractional luminosity,
1419
fdisk, and BB radius, RMBB, in case of an MBB model (Sect. 5.2).
1420
A set of new parameter values was randomly generated from
1421
which we calculated the SED. This led to a new χ2 value that
1422
was compared to the previous minimum value. The χ2 parame-
1423
ter estimates how likely the set of parameter values fits the SED.
1424
If the probability was larger than a certain threshold value, the
1425
set was saved. In the end, it was counted how often the code
1426
reached a certain set of fdisk and RMBB. The closer the parame-
1427
ters get to the best-fitting values, the higher the probability. The
1428
resulting distribution in parameter space represents an estimate
1429
for the probability distribution of the parameters and thus, allows
1430
us to calculate the confidence levels for the parameters assuming
1431
that the values follow a normal distribution in parameter space
1432
(simulated annealing; e.g., Pawellek 2017).
1433

5.3. Results of MBB modeling
1478
The results of the one-component MBB fitting are specified in
1479
Table E.3. Based on the criteria outlined in Sect. 5.1, fourteen
1480
SEDs were fitted using a two-component model, incorporating
1481
both warm and cold dust components. The best-fit parameters
1482
from this modeling are provided in Table E.4. Figure D.1 (top
1483
row) shows examples of SEDs fitted with MBB models consist-
1484
ing of one or two components. Using the results of these fits, we
1485
investigated the evolution of the disk IR excess, the correlation
1486
between the estimated dust mass in the belt and its radial dis-
1487
tance from the star for A-type and F-type stars in our sample, as
1488
well as the correlation between dust mass and host star mass for
1489
all targets.
1490
The disk IR excess is one of the key factors influencing disk
1491
detectability (Sect. 6). Younger debris disks exhibit higher frac-
1492
tional luminosities, as their luminosity scales with dust mass,
1493
which is at its peak in early evolutionary stages. Various dust re-
1494
moval mechanisms, including the blowout of the smallest dust
1495
particles due to radiation pressure, the Poynting-Robertson drag,
1496
and photoevaporation, play a crucial role in shaping the evolu-
1497
tion of disk mass and luminosity (e.g., Dominik & Decin 2003).
1498
Previous studies (e.g., Kalas et al. 2000; Rieke et al. 2005) have
1499
shown that the fractional luminosities of debris disks, fdisk, and
1500
consequently their dust content, follow a power-law dependence
1501
on time
1502
fdisk ∝t αt
age.
1503
Figure 12 shows the evolution of disk fractional luminosity
1504
for the debris disks around A- and F-type stars in our sample.
1505
By fitting the data points in the [fdisk, tage] parameter space, we
1506
derived the power-law indices αt = −1.18±0.14 for A-type stars
1507
and αt = −0.81 ± 0.12 for F-type stars. These exponents are
1508
in general agreement, though formally distinct within 1σ uncer-
1509
tainties with the steady-state collisional evolution theory of plan-
1510
etesimal belts, which predicts a disk luminosity decline propor-
1511
tional to t−1
age (Dominik & Decin 2003). We note that the αt = −1
1512
slope (black dash-dotted line in Fig. 12) is only valid for colli-
1513
sional systems in steady-state, where the disk age exceeds the
1514
collisional lifetimes of the largest planetesimals it contains. As
1515
pointed out by Löhne et al. (2008), this condition might be met
1516
only in very old systems (> 1 Gyr), or in very massive disks
1517
and/or disks at short radial distances from their host stars. Given
1518
that the median age of our sample is ∼100 Myr, and the median
1519
belt distances are around 70−80 au, our results do not align with
1520
these expectations.
1521
Our results show however that debris disks around A-type
1522
stars tend to decline more rapidly in brightness than those around
1523
F-type stars. This behavior can be interpreted in the context
1524
of differences in initial disk masses and their influence on col-
1525
lisional evolution timescales. Debris disks with higher masses
1526
produce dust more efficiently due to the increased frequency of
1527
planetesimal collisions (e.g., Wyatt 2008). However, these sys-
1528
tems also deplete more rapidly, since both large planetesimals
1529
and the dust generated by their collisions are removed more
1530
quickly, either through further collisional grinding or via radia-
1531
tive forces acting on small grains (Löhne et al. 2008; Krivov
1532
2010). A-type stars, being more massive and luminous than F-
1533
type stars, are expected to host more massive planetesimal belts,
1534
which evolve faster due to the enhanced dynamical excitation
1535
and stronger radiation pressure that efficiently clears small grains
1536
from the system. In contrast, the more gradual decay of IR ex-
1537
cess observed in F-type systems suggests slower collisional pro-
1538
cessing, consistent with initially less massive disks and weaker
1539
dynamical stirring.
1540
To further investigate this trend and quantify the effect of
1541
stellar mass on debris disk evolution, we estimated dust masses
1542
for our targets using the MBB best-fit parameters and equation

ity of the debris disks detected in our sample are younger than

50 Myr (90% of all detections), we divided the sample into two

1555
To investigate the Mdust −M⋆relation and its potential evo-
1556
lution over time, we fitted a power-law function in log-log space
1557
log(Mdust/M⊕) = αmass log(M⋆/M⊙) + βmass to three datasets:
1558
disks in the 10 −50 Myr range, disks older than 50 Myr, and
1559
all disks older than 10 Myr. The best-fit parameters αmass and
1560
βmass for the three subsamples are reported in Table 3 (rows 3-5)
1561
and the fits are visualized in Fig. 13, along with 68% and 95%
1562
confidence intervals for the full sample. In all cases, we find a
1563
steeper than a linear relation (αmass > 1) between the estimated
1564
disk dust mass and the mass of the host star. Notably, the scaling
1565
relation for the younger group of debris disks (see row 3 of Ta-
1566
ble 3) is steeper than that of the older group (row 4 of Table 3).
1567
This decrease in steepness with age may indicate a more rapid
1568
dust mass depletion in initially more massive disks, a trend that
1569
is also apparent in Fig. 12.
1570
It is well established that PPD masses correlate with stel-
1571
lar mass as well (e.g., Andrews et al. 2013; Barenfeld et al.
1572
2016; Ansdell et al. 2016). For example, Pascucci et al. (2016)
1573
analyzed the scaling of disk masses with stellar mass in star-
1574
forming regions, using various assumptions for the dust tempera-
1575
ture–luminosity relation, and similarly found super-linear trends.
1576
To place our findings in context, we included in Table 3 (rows
1577
1 −2) the best-fit parameters reported by Pascucci et al. (2016)
1578
for the Chamaeleon I star-forming region and overplot the cor-
1579
responding fits in Fig. 13 as black solid lines. As evident from
1580
this figure, the PPD relation is very similar to that we found for
1581
the debris disks and supports the idea that debris disks reflect
1582
the initial conditions set during the protoplanetary phase. This
1583
is particularly significant, as PPD masses represent the material
1584
reservoir available for planet formation. A super-linear scaling
1585
of disk mass with stellar mass therefore implies that the result-
1586
ing planet populations, both in terms of typical masses and oc-
1587
currence rates, are also expected to have a positive correlation
1588
with stellar mass.
1589
Comparing the offset of the linear relations (Col. 4 in Ta-
1590
ble 3) between PPD and debris disk dust masses (Fig. 13) we
1591
find that the average dust mass decreases by ∼3 dex within the
1592
first 50 Myr, and by 3.7 dex for the older stars in our sample.
1593
However, the dust masses exhibit a relatively large spread, which
1594
can be partially attributed to the variation in belt radii among the
1595
systems with the same mass of the host star, and scaling the disk
1596
mass with its radius.
1597
A correlation between dust mass and radial location is con-
1598
sistent with previous studies suggesting that belts at larger dis-
1599
tances from the star are likely to be more massive (e.g., Andrews
1600
et al. 2013; Matrà et al. 2025). This trend can be explained by
1601
the fact that a wider belt spans a larger volume, thereby allowing
1602
for a greater population of dust-producing planetesimals, assum-
1603
ing a roughly constant surface density or collisional activity per
1604
unit area. Moreover, the collisional timescales in outer regions
1605
of debris disks are longer due to lower orbital velocities and de-
1606
creased dynamical stirring, allowing dust to persist for extended
1607
periods and accumulate to a higher levels (e.g., Wyatt 2008).

We considered grain sizes ranging from a minimum value, amin,

1672

1673
ticles absorb and emit radiation efficiently only at wavelengths
1674

1675

1676

1708
With our chosen approach, we focused on spatially resolved
1709
data from scattered-light images. However, warm components
1710
that remain undetected in the images or are spatially unre-
1711
solved are not included in the modeling process. As a result, the
1712
NIR/MIR data of such SEDs are not well-fitted, and part of the
1713
emission that could originate from a warm dust component is in-
1714
stead incorporated into the fit of the cold dust belt. In such cases,
1715
the fitted minimum grain size is underestimated.
1716
To mitigate this issue, for targets with a sufficiently large
1717
number of available photometric points, we tested various fit-
1718
ting approaches. These included a fit with a warm component
1719
modeled as a BB, which provided an upper limit on amin, and a
1720
fit excluding the warm component, which yielded a lower limit
1721
on amin. These approaches allowed us to derive a mean value for
1722
the minimum grain size, along with its upper and lower bound-
1723
aries. For targets, where a warm component fit was not feasible,
1724
only lower limits on the minimum grain size were obtained.

where ⟨Qpr⟩is the mean radiation pressure coupling coefficient 1746
averaged over the stellar flux (e.g., Augereau et al. 1999), G is 1747
the gravitational constant, and c is the speed of light. We as- 1748

1754
The derived minimum grain sizes, or their lower limits in 1755
cases where the warm component could not be fitted, are pre- 1756
sented in Fig. 15, while the ratios of these values to the blowout 1757
grain sizes are shown in Fig. 16 as a function of stellar lumi- 1758
nosity. Notably, nearly all derived amin values are close to, but 1759
consistently larger than, the corresponding blowout sizes ablow. 1760
This is expected, as grains smaller than ablow are efficiently re- 1761
moved from the systems on short timescales due to radiation 1762
pressure. Figure 16 reveals that the ratio amin/ablow tends to in- 1763
crease with decreasing stellar luminosity, reaching values of up 1764
to ∼9 in the case of HD 105. This trend corroborates earlier find- 1765
ings (see Pawellek et al. 2014; Pawellek & Krivov 2015) and 1766
could have several, not mutually exclusive, explanations: limita- 1767
tions in grain surface energy that inhibit the production of small 1768
collisional fragments (Krijt & Kama 2014; Thebault 2016) or 1769
lower dynamical excitation levels in cold disks, leading to a de- 1770
pletion of small grains (Thébault & Wu 2008).
1771
For nearly all systems, the derived q values (Col. 4 in Ta- 1772
ble 4) are close to the canonical value of 3.5, which is ex- 1773
pected for an idealized infinite, self-similar collisional cascade 1774
(Dohnanyi 1969). The mean value of q = 3.62 is slightly higher 1775
(Fig. 17), which is consistent with expectations for more realistic 1776
collisional systems, where the critical specific energy required 1777
for fragmentation increases with decreasing particle size in the 1778
strength-dominated regime (O’Brien & Greenberg 2003).

1812
6. Detections versus non-detections
1813
To comprehensively understand the diversity of debris disk ar-
1814
chitectures, it is essential not only to analyze systems where
1815
belts are detected in scattered light, but also to interpret cases
1816
of non-detections, which provide valuable constraints. They may
1817
indicate disks that are intrinsically fainter, narrower, more pole-
1818
on, or more evolved, and thus consistent with lower dust masses
1819
or smaller planetesimal belts located closer to the star. Non-
1820
detections in scattered light may still host massive disks visible
1821
in the IR, and their lack of scattered-light visibility could be ex-
1822
plained by viewing geometry or dust properties. Additionally,
1823
the observing conditions during the runs, such as atmospheric
1824
seeing, coherence time, or instrument stability, can significantly
1825
influence detection sensitivity, particularly for faint disks.
1826
Two-thirds of the debris disks in our sample were not de-
1827
tected in SPHERE observations. Therefore, in the following sec-
1828
tions, we use the results of disk and SED modeling to explore
1829
the potential causes of these non-detections in more detail. Par-
1830
ticular attention is given to the optical properties of dust grains,
1831
focusing on two complementary approaches for deriving dust
1832
scattering characteristics, such as albedo and maximum polar-
1833
ization fraction: one based on theoretical predictions using Mie
1834
theory (Mie 1908), and the other on measurements of total and
1835
polarized scattered fluxes.
1836
6.1. Disk luminosity and dust mass
1837
Debris disks with an IR excess below 10−4 are very faint, mak-
1838
ing them challenging to image in scattered light with current in-
1839
strumentation. The faintest debris disk successfully imaged with
1840
SPHERE is that of HD 141943 (Boccaletti et al. 2019), with a
1841
disk fractional luminosity of fdisk = 1.2 × 10−4.
1842
As illustrated in Fig. 12, where targets with detected disks are
1843
represented by filled circles, stellar age appears to be one of the
1844
most critical factors limiting the detectability of scattered light
1845
from debris disks around distant stars. Among all disks detected
1846
with SPHERE, 90% of the targets have a mean estimated age
1847
below 50 Myr. The stellar age histogram in Fig. 1 (lower left
1848
panel) also shows that debris disks are detected in more than
1849
50% of systems younger than 100 Myr, whereas for older stars,
1850
the detection rate drops to just 5%. This declining detection rate
1851
with increasing stellar age can be attributed to the progressive
1852
reduction in dust mass over time, leading to a decrease in both
1853
scattered and thermal emission from dust particles (e.g., Wyatt
1854
2008; Krivov 2010).
1855
The relative small number of detected debris disks older than
1856
50 Myr in our sample, which spans system ages from a few Myr
1857
to several Gyr, may provide constraints on the size of the largest
1858
planetesimals formed by the end of the PPD phase. To address
1859
the issue of overly high inferred debris disk masses, Krivov &
1860
Wyatt (2021) proposed that young debris disks may contain rel-
1861
atively small largest planetesimals (on the order of 1 km in size),
1862
suggesting that “planetesimals are born small”. The detection
1863
statistics in our sample support this hypothesis, as disks formed
1864
with small planetesimals are expected to appear bright at young
1865
ages (tens of Myr) but fade rapidly within a few hundred Myr,
1866
whereas disks formed with larger planetesimals would maintain
1867
their brightness over several Gyr.
1868
6.2. Disk geometry and observing techniques
1869
In addition to system age, the viewing geometry of the disk and
1870
the observing technique significantly influence the detectability
1871
of debris disks through DI. In particular, disk inclination often
1872
plays a decisive role, as highly inclined (nearly edge-on) sys- 1873
tems are generally easier to detect (e.g., Esposito et al. 2020). 1874
Such disks exhibit increased SB along the edges of the planetes- 1875
imal belt, where the column density of dust particles is highest, 1876
and on the disk’s front side, where forward-scattering enhances 1877
the intensity of scattered light. These effects make inclined disks 1878
more readily observable, whereas pole-on systems, which lack 1879
strong forward-scattering features and appear more diffuse, are 1880
inherently more challenging to detect.
1881
To illustrate this effect, we generated model images of a typ- 1882
ical debris disk observed at inclinations 0◦(pole-on), 45◦and 1883
90◦(edge-on). For this purpose we used the model described in 1884
Sect. 4.5.1 adopting parameters commonly found in disk stud- 1885
ies. Figure 19 presents the corresponding scattered light images, 1886
with total intensity shown in the top row and polarized inten- 1887
sity in the bottom row. When viewed edge-on (top left panel), 1888
the disk exhibits the highest SB, significantly enhancing its de- 1889
tectability.
1890
Additionally, the observing technique most commonly used 1891
for imaging debris disks in scattered light is the ADI (Marois 1892
et al. 2006). This method is particularly sensitive to edge-on 1893
systems, as they generate a signal that differs from the stel- 1894
lar PSF when the sky field rotates. For disks with lower incli- 1895
nations (i ≲70◦), ADI is less effective, and for rotationally 1896
symmetric pole-on disks, it is inapplicable. As a result, despite 1897
their high IR excesses (fdisk > 10−3), the debris disks around 1898
HD 107146 and HD 95086 remained undetected in the SPHERE 1899
ADI datasets. Nonetheless, a large sky rotation angle during ob- 1900
servations under good conditions can improve the detectability 1901
of low-inclination disks. This is demonstrated in the case of 1902
HD 105 debris disk (Fig. 2), which was successfully detected, 1903
even though it has a relatively low inclination of 50.5◦.
1904
For imaging debris disks at low inclinations, PDI is more 1905
suitable than ADI. PDI yields images of the polarized intensity 1906
of scattered light, referred to as polarimetric images, which com- 1907
plement total intensity images of the same disk. However, the 1908
SB distribution in polarimetric images differs from that in to- 1909
tal intensity images, as the two are governed by distinct phase 1910
functions: the SPF and the pSPF, respectively. This distinction is 1911
illustrated in Fig. 19, which shows simulated images of total and 1912
polarized intensities for a debris disk generated using our model 1913
with a parametric representation of the phase functions: a HG 1914
function with an asymmetry parameter of g = 0.6 for the SPF, 1915
and the function given by Eq. 2 with a maximum polarization 1916
fraction pmax = 0.3 for the pSPF.
1917
The total flux, or integrated intensity, defined as the sum over 1918
all image pixels containing disk emission, also differs between 1919
total scattered and polarized intensity images and varies sys- 1920
tematically with disk inclination. This effect is demonstrated in 1921
Fig. 20, which is based on the same model as in Fig. 19. The left 1922
panel of Fig. 20 shows the total scattered flux, Fsca, while the 1923
middle panel displays the total polarized flux, Fpol, both plot- 1924
ted as functions of the scattering asymmetry parameter g and 1925
disk inclination. Each flux is normalized by a factor of 4π/Lsca, 1926
where Lsca denotes the scattered luminosity of the disk, or total 1927
intensity integrated over the full solid angle.
1928
As expected, the total scattered flux reaches a maximum for 1929
an edge-on disk with g = 0.9, corresponding to strongly forward- 1930
scattering grains (Fig. 20 left panel). The maximum polarized 1931
flux is obtained for a pole-on disk with an isotropic scattering 1932
parameter of g = 0 (Fig. 20 middle panel). In this configura- 1933
tion, most scattering occurs at θ = 90◦, where, according to 1934
our model, the degree of linear polarization reaches its maxi- 1935
mum (Eq. 3). However, the polarized scattered intensity repre- 1936
sents only a fraction of the total scattered intensity (Fig. 20 right 1937
panel), and this fraction can decrease rapidly with lower disk in- 1938
clination, particularly if the dust particles exhibit strong forward- 1939
scattering behavior. A comparison of the model images in Fig. 19 1940
and Fig. 20 demonstrate that debris disks appear faintest in po- 1941
larized intensity when viewed pole-on, and that they consistently 1942

exhibit lower brightness in polarized light than in total scattered
1943
light, irrespective of the dust’s optical properties.
1944
Despite this, thirty six debris disks in our sample were suc-
1945
cessfully detected using the PDI modes of IRDIS and ZIMPOL
1946
(Fig. 5). The PDI data processing methodology enables a more
1947
effective subtraction of the stellar PSF from the science frames
1948
compared to the ADI technique. As a result, polarimetric images
1949
can achieve higher contrast levels, reaching up to 10−8 −10−7
1950
with ZIMPOL (Hunziker et al. 2020; Tschudi et al. 2024). This
1951
allows for the easy detection of young, low-inclination disks
1952
with a high polarization fraction of scattered light, where dust
1953
particles are confined to a narrow, bright ring such as HD 181327
1954
planetesimal belt (Fig. 5; Milli et al. 2024). Conversely, older
1955
low-inclination disks, which exhibit a broad radial dust distribu-
1956
tion in some ALMA images and may consist of multiple faint
1957
planetesimal rings, are more challenging to resolve using PDI.
1958
Broad pole-on disks generally have a lower surface density re-
1959
sulting in lower SB in polarized light. This could explain the
1960
marginal detection of debris belts in HD 107146 using IRDIS
1961
H-band polarimetry, despite relatively favorable observing con-
1962
ditions.
1963
6.3. Influence and derivation of dust albedo and polarization
1964
characteristics
1965
The optical properties of dust particles, specifically their scat- 1966
tering and absorption efficiencies, can also be responsible for a 1967
non-detection of a debris disk. If the dust scattering efficiency is 1968
low at a particular wavelength, the SB of the debris disk is cor- 1969
respondingly low at that wavelength, decreasing the probability 1970
of disk detection.
1971
The optical characteristics of dust grains are fundamental pa- 1972
rameters in the study of circumstellar environments. They are in- 1973
trinsically linked to the grains’ composition, structure, and SD, 1974
and thus provide indirect constraints on the primordial solid- 1975
phase reservoir from which exoplanets may have formed. Un- 1976
derstanding these properties is therefore a key objective in debris 1977
disk research.
1978
One approach to constraining dust composition is through 1979
the evaluation of the dust albedo, which quantifies the rela- 1980
tive efficiency of scattering versus total extinction (scattering 1981
plus absorption). A higher albedo than 0.5 indicates that scat- 1982
tering dominates over absorption, while a lower albedo suggests 1983
that absorption is more significant. If the dust grain SD within 1984
a disk is known, often inferred from modeling the SED (e.g., 1985
Pawellek et al. 2019) or scattered-light imaging (e.g., Olofsson 1986
et al. 2016), then theoretical predictions of albedo can be made 1987
for various dust compositions using Mie theory (Mie 1908) or 1988
Article number, page 27 of 57


## Page 28


A&A proofs: manuscript no. output
more complex models accounting for grain porosity and non-
1989
sphericity (Draine & Flatau 1994; Min et al. 2005).
1990
These theoretical predictions can then be compared to ob-
1991
servational constraints, derived from combined measurements of
1992
scattered stellar light and thermal re-emission (SED). By jointly
1993
analyzing these datasets, the range of plausible dust composi-
1994
tions can be narrowed down. For example, grains composed pri-
1995
marily of astronomical silicates, carbonaceous materials, or ices
1996
each exhibit distinct scattering and absorption efficiencies, and
1997
thus different albedo values. This comparison allows to exclude
1998
certain grain compositions and structures, providing a more re-
1999
fined picture of the physical nature of dust in debris disks.
2000
In the following section, we present the methodology used
2001
to calculate the albedo of dust grains in debris disks, employing
2002
Mie theory to derive the scattering and absorption efficiencies
2003
for particles of specified composition and size. We then describe
2004
how these theoretical predictions are compared with observa-
2005
tional constraints, obtained from the analysis of scattered-light
2006
images of spatially resolved debris disks in our sample and their
2007
IR excess measurements.
2008

6.3.2. Scattering albedo of various dust compositions
2042
Using Eq. 10, we modeled the spectral albedo for a range of dust 2043
compositions, including astrosilicates, amorphous carbon, and 2044
silicate grains coated with either dirty or water ice, motivated 2045
by both observational evidence and theoretical considerations. 2046
Astrosilicates are widely used to represent the dominant silicate 2047
emission features observed in mid-IR spectra of circumstellar 2048
environments and reflect the mineralogical composition inferred 2049
from both debris disks and Solar System dust populations (e.g., 2050
Draine 2003a; Dorschner et al. 1995). Amorphous carbon is in- 2051
cluded to represent more absorbing, featureless materials, com- 2052
monly invoked to model the continuum emission in disks and 2053
supported by the presence of carbonaceous material in interplan- 2054
etary dust and meteorites (e.g., Zubko et al. 1996; Li & Green- 2055
berg 1997). To account for conditions in the outer, colder regions 2056
of disks, we also considered silicate grains coated with water ice, 2057
which are expected beyond the ice line and significantly modify 2058
scattering properties due to their high albedo and distinct optical 2059
constants (e.g., Donaldson et al. 2013; Xie et al. 2025). Addition- 2060
ally, dirty ice grains, incorporating refractory inclusions, provide 2061
a more realistic representation of ice mantles processed by col- 2062
lisions and irradiation (e.g., Preibisch et al. 1993; Li & Green- 2063
berg 1998). This set of compositions spans a physically plausible 2064
range and enables the exploration of how material properties in- 2065
fluence key observables such as scattered light brightness.
2066
For these four dust compositions, we computed the scattering 2067
albedo ωλ and present the results as 2D maps in Fig. 21. The 2068
calculations are based on a grain SD model n(a)da ∝a−qda, 2069
with grain sizes spanning the range amin ⩽a ⩽amax. We varied 2070
amin between 0.9 and 5 µm while keeping amax fixed at 5 mm, 2071
following our SED fitting procedure (Sect. 5.4). We considered 2072
three values for the SD power-law exponent, q = 3.0, 3.5 and 2073
4.0, assuming that for most debris systems, the exponent falls 2074
within this range (see Col. 4 in Table 4).
2075
The optical data for astrosilicates were taken from Draine 2076
(2003a), for water ice from Warren & Brandt (2008), and for 2077
amorphous carbon and dirty ice from Preibisch et al. (1993). 2078
The refractive indices of silicate grains coated with water or 2079
dirty ice were calculated assuming a volume fraction of 50% 2080
for each component. This corresponds to a mass fraction of 79% 2081
for silicates and 21% for water ice, based on their material den- 2082
sities of ϱsil = 3.5 g cm−3 and ϱice = 0.92 g cm−3, respectively 2083
(Pollack et al. 1994). The optical constants of the dirty ice coat- 2084
ing were derived for a mixture of H2O- and NH3-ices with a 2085
volume ratio of 3:1 polluted by amorphous carbon with a vol- 2086
ume fraction of 10%. Adopting the material densities for carbon 2087
ϱcar = 2.3 g cm−3 and for NH3-ice ϱNH3 ice = 0.85 g cm−3, we 2088
obtained a mass fraction of 23% for the dirty ice mantle.
2089
The left column in Fig. 21 presents the single scattering 2090
albedo for particles composed of pure astrosilicates. For q = 3.0 2091
the albedo remains nearly constant (∼0.56) for all SDs and 2092
wavelengths, corresponding to a gray disk, meaning that the re- 2093
flectance spectrum of the disk does not vary with wavelength. 2094
For q = 4.0, the spectral variation of ωλ is more significant, 2095
with the albedo increasing towards the lower right corner of the 2096
plot as the effective size parameter decreases xeff. This trend is 2097

6.3.3. Detection of the HD 181327 debris belt versus
2143
non-detection of the inner belt around HD 107146
2144
The difference in scattering efficiency of the dust material may 2145
explain the detection of the HD 181327 debris belt and the non- 2146
detection of the inner belt around HD 107146 in the H-band po- 2147
larimetric observations with IRDIS. Both stars were observed 2148
using the same instrumental setup, under comparable observing 2149
conditions, and with nearly identical total exposure times.
2150
The F6V star HD 181327 (L⋆= 2.88 L⊙, 18 −23 Myr, 2151
fdisk = (2.6 ± 0.7) × 10−3) hosts a debris belt at a radial distance 2152
of 82 au, inclined at 30◦(Table 2). As mentioned in Sect. 5.5, 2153
the G2V star HD 107146 (L⋆= 1.04 L⊙, 50 −2400 Myr, fdisk = 2154
(1.1 ± 0.3) × 10−3) possesses two low-inclination (i = 19◦) plan- 2155
etesimal belts located at ∼50 and 120 au. The stellar illumination 2156
of the HD 181327 belt is comparable to that of the inner belt of 2157
HD 107146, as it is governed by the ratio L⋆/R2
belt, which yields 2158
a value of 0.55 Wm−2 in both cases. This is two orders of mag- 2159
nitude lower than the solar flux received by Jupiter in the Solar 2160
System. Additionally, the small difference in inclination between 2161
the two planetesimal belts is not expected to significantly impact 2162
the amount of observed polarized scattered light, as seen in the 2163
middle panel of Fig. 20. Thus, the stellar illumination and disk 2164
viewing geometry are not the primary factors responsible for the 2165
non-detection of the 50 au belt in the HD 107146 system, or at 2166
least, they do not play a decisive role.
2167
Using the results of SD modeling, we estimated the scatter- 2168
ing efficiencies of dust grains in both systems assuming they are 2169
composed of astrosilicates. Interestingly, for both systems we 2170
obtained the same SD power-law index of 3.42 but different min- 2171
imum grain sizes: amin = 1.00 ± 0.18 µm for the HD 181327 belt 2172
and amin = 2.79±1.17 µm for the HD 107146 belt. Such a differ- 2173
ence in minimum grain sizes would lead to different bulk albedo 2174
values in the H band (λc = 1.6 µm): ωH = 0.61 for HD 181327 2175

However, it is entirely possible that the dust particles in the

et al. 2014) to the polarized intensity contrasts measured with 2387
SPHERE.

2524

6.4. Observing conditions
2586
Due to the faint and extended nature of debris disks in scattered 2587
light, their detection is strongly influenced by both ambient con- 2588
ditions (e.g., coherence time, seeing, wind speed) and observa- 2589
tional parameters such as airmass, detector integration time (DIT 2590
or exposure time per frame), total exposure time, and, in the case 2591
of ADI, the total rotation angle of the sky field during the obser- 2592
vation. For successful DI of debris disks with SPHERE instru- 2593
ments, the coherence time should ideally exceed 3 −4 ms, while 2594
the seeing conditions should remain below 0.8′′. If the coherence 2595
time is short (< 3 ms), the stellar position behind the corona- 2596
graph can deviate by more than one pixel from the intended cen- 2597

ter position. Misalignment in individual frames reduces the S/N
2598
in the final science image after frame combination. Similarly,
2599
high atmospheric turbulence (seeing > 0.8′′) or strong variabil-
2600
ity in the PSF adversely impacts detection.
2601
This effect was notably observed during polarimetric obser-
2602
vations of HD 117214 with ZIMPOL, where the degrading see-
2603
ing conditions over the two-hour observing run significantly re-
2604
duced the detectability of the debris disk (Engler et al. 2020).
2605
When the seeing exceeds 0.95 −1′′, the stellar PSF often be-
2606
comes highly variable, making it unlikely to resolve a debris
2607
disk, even if the disk is intrinsically bright. However, in ADI
2608
observations, a sufficiently large sky rotation angle and a robust
2609
number of frames allow for selective frame combination, which
2610
can improve the S/N by applying thresholds on seeing (or equiv-
2611
alently PSF FWHM).
2612
The quality of data obtained with SPHERE instruments is
2613
also affected by wind speed at Paranal Observatory. The optimal
2614
wind speed range for observations lies between 2 and 5 m s−1.
2615
When wind speeds drop below 1 m s−1, data quality is impacted
2616
by the “low wind effect” (LWE; Milli et al. 2018). To mitigate
2617
this issue, a special low-emissivity coating for the telescope spi-
2618
ders was introduced in August 2017. Before this modification,
2619
SPHERE observations conducted at wind speeds below 4 m s−1
2620
were often degraded by the LWE. Conversely, high-altitude tur-
2621
bulence with wind speeds exceeding 5 m s−1 produces a wind-
2622
driven halo within the AO correction radius, thereby reducing
2623
achievable contrast (Cantalloube et al. 2018).
2624
Furthermore, debris disks observed at high airmass (> 1.7)
2625
are essentially undetectable. The angular size of the disk also
2626
plays a crucial role; it must fit within the FOV of the instrument
2627
or exceed its inner working angle. This requirement is not met
2628
for some targets in our sample, such as HD 3003, where the sus-
2629
pected debris disk around the primary star of this binary system
2630
likely has an angular size smaller than 0.1′′.
2631
From the analysis of detections and non-detections of debris
2632
disks, we conclude that numerous observational requirements
2633
must be fulfilled for successful imaging of debris disks in scat-
2634
tered light with SPHERE. Under optimal conditions, debris disks
2635
with low IR excess (∼10−4) can be imaged, whereas even bright 2636
disks (fdisk > 10−3) may remain undetected under suboptimal 2637
conditions. These considerations are critical for interpreting de- 2638
bris disk brightness in scattered light and estimating their scat- 2639
tering albedos, particularly when comparing different systems.
2640
7. Debris disks as integral components of stellar
2641
systems
2642
Along with stellar, substellar and planetary companions, debris 2643
disks represent key components of stellar systems, each trac- 2644
ing different aspects of their formation and dynamical evolution. 2645
Bright debris disks around young stars serve as visible signposts 2646
of ongoing or past planet formation, and their morphology of- 2647
fer insights into the dynamical environment of a system, often 2648
shaped by gravitational interactions with nearby massive bodies. 2649
Understanding how debris disks relate spatially and dynamically 2650
to both exoplanets and stellar companions is essential for build- 2651
ing a more complete picture of young planetary systems.
2652
In this section, we explore these relationships by examining 2653
debris disks detected with SPHERE in the context of the com- 2654
panions present around the stars in our sample. Among these 2655
systems are 23 young stars with confirmed exoplanets, discov- 2656
ered through various detection techniques, including DI, astrom- 2657
etry, transits, and radial velocity (RV) measurements. Table F.1 2658
lists the parameters of these exoplanets, including only those 2659
with masses below 13 MJup, the threshold below which compan- 2660
ions are categorized as planets. All other types of companions, 2661
both confirmed and candidates, are described in Appendix F and 2662
listed in Table F.2.
2663
7.1. Companions to the program stars
2664
The debris disks detected with SPHERE occupy a specific region 2665
within stellar systems, clearly distinct from the regions where 2666
stellar and planetary companions are found. This distinction is 2667
likely due to both the intrinsic characteristics of debris disks and 2668
selection effects that lead to their detection. To explore these, we 2669
considered the separation - mass plane presented in Fig. 27. In 2670
this figure, we compared the distribution of debris disks with that 2671
of companions to the stars listed in Appendix F.
2672
To interpret the locations of debris disks and compact com- 2673
panions around stars, we first briefly review the main selection 2674
effects at play. The FOV of SPHERE allows the detection of 2675
disks at projected separations of at least 0.1 −0.15′′ from the 2676
star. Disks located closer are obscured by the coronagraph and 2677
are difficult to detect due to the brightness of the stellar halo. 2678
Conversely, disks at separations beyond 5.5′′ may fall outside the 2679
instrument’s FOV, though they may still be detected if observed 2680
at a high inclination. Given that the distances to the program 2681
stars range from a few to over a hundred parsecs, the number of 2682
stars effectively surveyed for debris disks of a given physical size 2683
varies. This variation is illustrated by the background grayscale 2684
in Fig. 27. We note that the distribution of disk radii is narrower 2685
than the region where the survey is complete across the program 2686
stars, suggesting that an underlying physical cause, rather than 2687
observational bias alone, may be responsible.
2688
Regarding the distribution of stellar companions, very few 2689
are found at separations similar to those of the debris disks in 2690
this sample. However, we know that the range from a few to 2691
hundreds of au is populated by many stellar companions and is 2692
the peak of the distribution of stellar companions for solar and 2693
A-type stars (Duquennoy & Mayor 1991; Raghavan et al. 2010; 2694
De Rosa et al. 2014; Gratton et al. 2023b). The lack of such 2695
companions in our sample may reflect the SPHERE observation 2696
strategy, as targets with known companions in this separation 2697
range were excluded from the SHINE survey, and only shallow 2698
observations were performed when such systems were inadver- 2699
tently included (see Bonavita et al. 2022). However, stellar com- 2700

7.2. Architectures of individual planetary systems
2710
The spatial distribution of planetary companions relative to de-
2711
bris disks provides critical insights into the architecture and dy-
2712
namical history of planetary systems. In particular, the location
2713
of planets within or near debris belts can indicate past migration
2714
processes, sculpting effects, and zones of dynamical stability or
2715
instability. In the sample of stars observed with SPHERE, the
2716
planetary companions (Mp < 13 MJup) are located closer to their
2717
host stars than the resolved debris disks (Fig. 27). This separa-
2718
tion is expected from both formation models and observational
2719
biases, but it also carries important implications for the interpre-
2720
tation of system dynamics.
2721
Among the systems in our sample, only five (HD 39060,
2722
HD 106906, HD 114082, HD 197481, and HD 218396) have
2723
both debris disks resolved with SPHERE and confirmed plan-
2724
etary companions. In these cases, the relative radial positions of
2725
disks and planets are well constrained, allowing for a detailed in-
2726
vestigation of system architecture and the study of how planets
2727
shape and interact with circumstellar debris.
2728
Figure 28 presents the configurations of these five systems
2729
in comparison with the Solar System, highlighting both similari-
2730
ties and diversity in planet-disk arrangements. This comparative
2731
approach helps to identify trends and anomalies that can guide
2732
future searches and refine theoretical models. By analyzing these
2733
architectures, we can also place constraints on the potential loca-
2734
tions and masses of additional, as-yet-undiscovered planets that
2735
may reside between known companions and debris structures.
2736
HD 39060 (β Pic)
The HD 39060 disk is the most prominent 2737
debris disk among all targets, as it was the first to be directly 2738
imaged (Smith & Terrile 1984). It is highly asymmetric and ex- 2739
tends beyond 1000 au (e.g., Janson et al. 2021). This vast disk 2740
consists of two cold exo-Kuiper belts, both observed edge-on but 2741
with slightly different PAs (Golimowski et al. 2006; Ahmic et al. 2742
2009, this work). Additionally, several inner planetesimal belts 2743
are likely present (Okamoto et al. 2004; Wahhaj et al. 2003), 2744
though their exact radial positions and orientations remain un- 2745
certain; therefore they are not depicted in Fig. 28.
2746
Two giant planets reside in the inner regions of the disk: 2747
HD 39060 b, a ∼11 MJup planet located at ∼9 au (Lagrange et al. 2748
2010), and HD 39060 c, a ∼10 MJup at ∼3 au (Lagrange et al. 2749
2019). The latter is positioned near the radial distance from the 2750
star where the stellar incident flux matches the solar incident flux 2751
on Earth, I⊕. This location is indicated by the blue vertical line 2752
in the right panel of Fig. 28, which compares the radial posi- 2753
tions of exoplanets in terms of the incident flux or irradiance I⋆
2754
from their host stars. For the two planets, a similar irradiance 2755
level suggests that their equilibrium temperatures, Teq (or BB- 2756
equivalent temperatures), are comparable, given that Teq ∼I1/4
⋆.
2757
HD 218396 (HR 8799) The planetary system of HD 218396 ex- 2758
hibits an even stronger resemblance to the Solar System in terms 2759
of stellar irradiance levels experienced by its giant exoplanets 2760
compared to the Jovian planets (Fig. 28 right panel). Its archi- 2761
tectural structure, featuring a warm belt (Rbelt ∼15 au), a cold 2762
belt (Rbelt ∼180 au), and four giant planets residing in the space 2763
between them, closely parallels the Solar System’s main asteroid 2764
belt, Edgeworth-Kuiper belt, and the orbits of the Jovian planets 2765
in between (Marois et al. 2010).
2766
As discussed in Sect. 4, the polarized intensity image taken 2767
in the H band with IRDIS reveals the warm belt spatially re- 2768
solved for the first time (Fig. 9i). Its radial position, indicated in 2769
left panel of Fig. 28, is close to that of planet HR 8799 e, which 2770
has a projected separation of 0.39′′ ± 0.01′′ (∼16 au) in the same 2771
dataset. This spatial coincidence hints at a possible role for the 2772
planet in shaping the inner belt’s edge or maintaining its struc- 2773
ture through dynamical shepherding.

HD 114082
HD 114082 is a young F3V star belonging to the
2789

2790

2791

The system HD 114082 offers a compelling example of a 2829
planetary system where giant planets orbit close to the host star, 2830
while a spatially extended debris disk lies much farther out and 2831
appears misaligned with the inner planetary orbits, so that their 2832
planes are inclined by at least 6-7 degrees relative to the disk 2833
plane.
2834
Such orbital misalignment between planets and debris disks 2835
have important implications for the dynamical history of the sys- 2836
tem. Several mechanisms have been proposed to explain such 2837
configurations. One possibility is planet–planet scattering, where 2838
gravitational interactions between giant planets lead to the ejec- 2839
tion of one or more bodies and a reconfiguration of the survivors 2840
onto eccentric and inclined orbits. This process can disrupt the 2841
coplanar architecture established during the PPD phase and has 2842
been invoked to explain the high eccentricities and inclinations 2843
observed in many giant exoplanets (e.g., Chatterjee et al. 2008; 2844
Juri´c & Tremaine 2008; Raymond et al. 2011).
2845
Another mechanism is secular perturbations from additional, 2846
possibly undetected, massive companions. If a distant planet is 2847
present on an inclined orbit, it can induce long-term precession 2848
in the orbits of inner planets or in the disk itself, leading to grad- 2849
ual misalignment over time. This mechanism, particularly the 2850
Kozai-Lidov effect, has been shown to cause significant orbital 2851
inclination variations under specific conditions (e.g., Nagasawa 2852
et al. 2008; Naoz et al. 2013).
2853
In the case of HD 114082, the wide separation between the 2854
planetary system and the debris belt, more than a factor of 30 2855
in radius, creates a dynamical environment in which such long- 2856
term perturbations are plausible. Furthermore, the large radial 2857
cavity separating the planets from the debris disk may itself be 2858
sculpted by additional low-luminosity companions or represent 2859
the aftermath of dynamical clearing by now-ejected bodies.
2860

The HD 114082 system offers a compelling case for inves-
2861
tigating the early dynamical evolution of tightly packed plane-
2862
tary systems accompanied by outer debris structures. Although
2863
TESS has detected single transit events for both planets, their
2864
orbital geometries remain poorly constrained due to the limited
2865
transit coverage, and the PAs of their orbital planes are still un-
2866
known. Continued photometric and spectroscopic monitoring of
2867
HD 114082 is essential to refine the orbital parameters of the
2868
planets, including their mutual orientation and orbital eccentric-
2869
ities.
2870
In particular, precisely determining the transit timing would
2871
enable targeted RV observations during the planet’s passage
2872
across the stellar disk. This could allow for the detection of the
2873
Rossiter–McLaughlin effect (Triaud 2018), a spectroscopic sig-
2874
nature that occurs when a transiting planet temporarily blocks
2875
part of the rotating stellar surface. Measuring this effect provides
2876
the sky-projected angle between the planetary orbital axis and
2877
the stellar spin axis, offering insight into the mutual alignment
2878
(or misalignment) between the planetary orbital planes and the
2879
host star’s equatorial plane. This, in turn, helps to reconstruct the
2880
three-dimensional architecture of the system and test scenarios
2881
of planet migration or dynamical perturbation.
2882
HD 197481 (AU Mic)
HD 197481 is an active flaring M1Ve
2883
star belonging to the βPMG and hosts a highly dynamical de-
2884
bris system. A monitoring campaign of AU Mic conducted with
2885
SPHERE between 2015 and 2017 revealed multiple dust clumps
2886
appearing above and below the disk midplane (Boccaletti et al.
2887
2018), seemingly moving in non-Keplerian orbits around the
2888
star. The origin of these fast-moving dust structures is still de-
2889
bated. One possibility is that they are generated in a collisional
2890
avalanche, where the main planetesimal belt intersects a de-
2891
bris stream resulting from the catastrophic disruption of a large
2892
asteroid-like body (Chiang & Fung 2017). Another explanation
2893
suggests that these dust clumps are expelled from the system by
2894
AU Mic’s strong stellar wind, having been emitted by a parent
2895
body, such as a planet or a disk substructure within the main
2896
debris belt (Sezestre et al. 2017). Indeed, AU Mic is known to
2897
host three confirmed planets that follow close orbits, possibly in
2898
a 4:6:9 mean-motion resonance (see Table F.1; Plavchan et al.
2899
2020; Martioli et al. 2021; Wittrock et al. 2023). However, they
2900
are too close to the star to be responsible for the observed dust
2901
clumps (Boccaletti 2023). In Fig. 28, these planets are shown in-
2902
side two dust belts, positioned at the locations of radial SB peaks
2903
as measured along the disk’s major axis in the r2-scaled H-band
2904
image from May 20, 2017. The AU Mic’s morphology, however,
2905
varies over time and resembles an edge-on spiral structure more
2906
than a stable belt system.
2907
The comparative architectures shown in Fig. 28 indicate that
2908
our Solar System is not unusual but instead falls within the gen-
2909
eral distribution of planetary systems hosting both debris disks
2910
and planets. In most benchmark systems, the giant planets are
2911
confined to the region inside the exo-Kuiper belt, typically span-
2912
ning orbital radii from a few to a few tens of au, while the plan-
2913
etesimal belts extend from several tens to more than one hun-
2914
dred au. An exception is HD 106906, where a massive (13 MJup)
2915
companion resides well outside the belt. When scaled by stellar
2916
irradiance (right panel in Fig. 28), the exo-Kuiper belt locations
2917
cluster around the equivalent solar-system value, underscoring
2918
the analogy. This alignment demonstrates that multi-planet ar-
2919
rangements inside wide Kuiper-belt analogs are common, and
2920
that the Solar System’s configuration, with its giant planets lo-
2921
cated interior to an outer cold belt, represents a recurring out-
2922
come of planetary system formation and evolution.
2923
7.3. Theoretical predictions for the masses of undetected
2924
planets
2925
To investigate the potential planetary architectures in other sys- 2926
tems with detected debris disks in our sample, we applied a set 2927
of analytical prescriptions under the assumption that observed 2928
gaps and edges of planetesimal belts have a dynamical origin. 2929
The adopted method depends on whether the system hosts a sin- 2930
gle resolved belt or multiple belts. For all systems, we assumed 2931
a radial belt width equal to 20% of its resolved central radius in 2932
consistency with our SED modeling approach (Sect. 5.4).
2933
For systems with only one resolved belt, we estimated the 2934
range of planet masses and semimajor axes for a single planet on 2935
a circular orbit capable of sculpting the inner edge of the belt. We 2936
assumed that such a planet dynamically clears a region interior 2937
to the belt by gravitationally ejecting dust particles. To relate 2938
the width of the cleared zone, ∆a, to the planet’s mass, Mp, we 2939
adopted the expression derived by Morrison & Malhotra (2015): 2940
∆a = 1.7
 Mp
M⋆
!0.31
ap,
2941
where ap is the semimajor axis of the planet and M⋆is the mass 2942
of the host star. Planet masses were explored in the range of 2943
0.1 −13 MJup, with corresponding semimajor axes calculated to 2944
match the observed belt edge. The resulting minimum and max- 2945
imum allowed semimajor axes, corresponding to the assumed 2946
mass bounds, are reported in Table 6.
2947
For systems with two or more resolved belts, we examined 2948
the gap between each pair of belts, testing dynamical config- 2949
urations involving one, two, or three planets on circular orbits 2950
capable of maintaining the observed separations. This analysis 2951
follows the framework of Lazzoni et al. (2018). In multi-planet 2952
scenarios, we assumed equal-mass planets arranged in a maxi- 2953
mally packed configuration, thus with the minimum orbital spac- 2954
ing required for long-term dynamical stability, in order to re- 2955
duce degeneracy in the possible solutions. For each configura- 2956
tion, the resulting planet masses and semimajor axes are reported 2957
in Table 7. For HD 141943, only the single-planet scenario is re- 2958
ported, as the resulting mass required to account for the observed 2959
belt structure is already extremely low (5 × 10−6 MJup), making 2960
the consideration of multi-planet solutions unnecessary.
2961
The results of our dynamical analysis suggest that many de- 2962
bris disk systems may host planetary companions whose gravi- 2963
tational perturbations shape the observed disk morphology. This 2964
is particularly evident in the case of HD 218396 (HR 8799). In a 2965
three-planet configuration with equal-mass planets of 6.78 MJup, 2966
our model yields a configuration that closely approximates the 2967
known planetary architecture of this system (Sect. 7.2), albeit 2968
without reproducing it in full detail.
2969
For
three
other
systems,
HD 36546,
HD 92945
and 2970
HD 120326, we find that a single planet located in the gap 2971
between two resolved belts must possess a mass greater than 2972
3.3 MJup, to account for the observed structure. This mass range 2973
lies within the detection capabilities of current HCI instruments. 2974
In the case of HD 92945, contrast limits achieved in IRDIS and 2975
IFS observations on 27 January 2018 were analyzed by Mesa 2976
et al. (2021). Using AMES-COND evolutionary models (Allard 2977
et al. 2012) to convert IRDIS contrast limits into mass con- 2978
straints, they derived mass limits between 1 and 2 MJup at the 2979
gap radial position. This comparison indicates that the observed 2980
gap is more likely carved by multiple lower-mass planets, al- 2981
though Marino et al. (2019) and Mesa et al. (2021) found that 2982
a single planet with a mass of 0.3 −0.6 MJup could reproduce 2983
the structure, assuming a narrower gap width of 20 au. In con- 2984
trast, our analysis adopts a gap width of 45.6 au (see Table 7), 2985
which would require a more massive perturber. According to our 2986
model, the masses of planets in a multi-planet configuration for 2987
the HD 92945 system would fall below the SPHERE detection 2988
limit of 1 MJup.

The SPHERE data for HD 120326 were investigated by Bon-
2990
nefoy et al. (2017), who discovered the double-belt structure
2991
around this star and identified ten candidate companions. Based
2992

2993
ison with earlier HST/STIS observations (Padgett & Stapelfeldt
2994

2995

2996

3002
In all other cases investigated, the predicted range of planet
3003
masses capable of sculpting the inner edges of single belts or
3004
clearing the gaps between two belts extends to values below the
3005
current detection limits of HCI surveys. This implies that a sig- 3006
nificant fraction of planetary systems could contain sub-Jovian 3007
or Neptune-like planets in wide orbits, which remain elusive to 3008
existing instruments but play a central role in shaping circum- 3009
stellar dust distributions.
3010
The inferred masses and orbital distances of these hypothet- 3011
ical planets in our analysis align well with the population of ex- 3012
oplanets identified through RV and transit surveys, especially 3013
in the super-Earth to sub-Saturn mass range (Winn & Petigura 3014
2020). These studies have shown that such planets are com- 3015
mon around a wide range of stellar types and may often occur 3016
in multi-planet configurations. The orbital distances we derive, 3017
typically tens to over a hundred au, complement these findings 3018
by probing an otherwise underexplored region of radial separa- 3019
tions. Our results further support the notion that debris disk mor- 3020
phology can serve as an indirect tracer of planetary companions 3021
(e.g., Raymond et al. 2011; Lee & Chiang 2016) and highlight 3022
the need for combined approaches incorporating disk modeling, 3023
DI, and indirect detection methods, to fully characterize the ar- 3024
chitectures of planetary systems.
3025
8. Summary
3026
In this work, we performed a demographics study of debris disks 3027
around young stars observed with SPHERE at optical and near- 3028
IR wavelengths. By analyzing a large sample of 161 targets with 3029
IR excess, we addressed morphological, photometric, and polari- 3030
metric properties of debris disks across different stellar types and 3031
ages. From this sample, compiled from archival GTO and open- 3032
time program observations, we resolve 40 disks in scattered light 3033
and 36 in polarized light, identifying seven systems with two 3034
planetesimal belts and two with three distinct belts (HD 131835 3035
and TWA 7). Newly resolved structures include the disks around 3036
HD 36968 and BD-20 951, as well as the inner belts of HR 8799 3037
and HD 36546.
3038
Using SPHERE images, we measured geometrical parame- 3039
ters of detected disks through ellipse fitting, and applied a grid of 3040
models for higher-quality data to derive disk radii, aspect ratios, 3041
radial density slopes, and scattering asymmetry parameters. This 3042
uniform modeling enables a consistent comparison of structural 3043
disk properties and reveals systematic dependencies of geomet- 3044
rical parameters on stellar luminosity. The inner slopes of grain 3045
density distributions steepen with increasing stellar luminosity, 3046
while disk vertical aspect ratios tend to decrease. For most sys- 3047
tems, aspect ratios are between 0.02 and 0.06, consistent with 3048
expectations for collisionally active belts, though gas-rich disks 3049
show unusually small values.
3050
A direct comparison between the radii measured in SPHERE 3051
scattered-light images and those derived from ALMA and 3052
SMA thermal-emission observations demonstrate a close spatial 3053
agreement, with a mean radius ratio of 1.05±0.04. In double-belt 3054
systems, the outer belts are typically 1.5−2 times larger than the 3055
inner ones.
3056
Almost all resolved planetesimal belts contain cold dust with 3057
BB temperatures below 100 K, with HD 172555 being the only 3058
exception. A weak correlation between belt radius and stellar 3059
luminosity is found, following Rbelt ∝L0.11±0.05
⋆
. When divid- 3060
ing belts according to dust temperatures associated with CO 3061
and CO2 freeze-out, this correlation becomes steeper: with α = 3062
0.30 ± 0.08 for CO subsample (Tbb < 35 K) and α = 0.30 ± 0.07 3063
for CO2 subsample (Tbb > 35 K). We also investigated how the 3064
locations of debris belts evolve with stellar age in relation to the 3065
possible migration of ice lines. For disks in the CO2 subsam- 3066
ple, we find that belt radii increase systematically with stellar 3067
age, following Rbelt ∝t0.37±0.11
age
, indicating that debris architec- 3068
tures evolve alongside stellar luminosity growth during the PMS 3069
phase.
3070
Complementary SED modeling with MBB and SD ap- 3071
proaches allowed us to connect disk morphology with photo- 3072

3074

3078
In addition, we examined the scaling of disk dust masses
3079
with stellar properties and compared them with relations estab-
3080
lished for PPD. The dust masses derived with MBB approach,
3081
scale super-linearly with stellar mass following Mdust ∝Mαmass
⋆
3082
with αmass = 1.6 ± 1.0 for systems aged 10–50 Myr and αmass =
3083
1.4 ± 0.9 for older disks, similar to the relations observed in
3084
2 −3 Myr old star-forming regions (e.g., Pascucci et al. 2016).
3085
This continuity suggests that the initial conditions set during
3086
the protoplanetary phase strongly influence debris disk evolu-
3087
tion. Furthermore, typical debris disk masses decrease by about
3088
three orders of magnitude within the first 50 Myr and by nearly
3089
four orders at later ages, consistent with collisional depletion.
3090
The Mdust −Rbelt relation also follows a power law with index
3091
larger than 2, implying that more extended belts tend to host pro-
3092
portionally larger dust reservoirs. Together, these trends demon-
3093
strate that debris disks preserve imprints of their primordial PPD
3094
phase while revealing the efficiency of collisional evolution in
3095
regulating dust content over time.
3096
To model the SEDs of the detected planetesimal belts with
3097
a SD approach, we adopted belt radii measured from SPHERE
3098
scattered-light images. The fits yield minimum grain sizes con-
3099
sistently larger than 0.8 µm and an average power-law slope of
3100
q = 3.62, slightly steeper than the canonical collisional cascade
3101
value of 3.5. The resulting dust masses of exo-Kuiper belts, in-
3102
tegrated over particle sizes from amin to 5 mm and assuming as-
3103
trosilicate composition, lie in the range 0.01 −1 M⊕and agree
3104
with those inferred from MBB modeling. Moreover, these dust
3105
masses scale approx. as R2.1
belt with radial distance in the sub-
3106
sample of A-, F-, and G-type stars with ages between 10 and
3107
200 Myr.
3108
Building on the SD modeling results, we estimated bulk dust
3109
albedo values using Mie theory for four different grain compo-
3110
sitions. The derived values are consistently higher than 0.5, but
3111
the variation between compositions is relatively small, indicating
3112
that dust albedo is unlikely to be the primary factor behind disk
3113
non-detections. In addition, we introduced in this work a para-
3114
metric approach based on image modeling and flux measure-
3115
ments from scattered-light and polarized-light images, demon-
3116
strating how both the dust albedo and the maximum polarization 3117
fraction can be derived with this method.
3118
Analysis of polarized-light images reveals a correlation be- 3119
tween polarized fluxes in H and J bands and modeled IR ex- 3120
cesses. The slope of this relation is shallower than that found for 3121
total-intensity optical images from HST, consistent with the fact 3122
that polarized flux traces only a fraction of total scattered light, 3123
dependent on dust properties and disk inclination.
3124
The analysis of non-detections further shows that 90% of the 3125
detected disks have estimated ages below 50 Myr, indicating that 3126
many undetected systems can be explained by intrinsically low 3127
dust masses resulting in faint scattered-light emission. Neverthe- 3128
less, even bright disks (fdisk > 10−3) may remain undetected in 3129
cases of unfavorable viewing geometry or suboptimal observ- 3130
ing conditions, whereas under optimal conditions disks with ex- 3131
cesses as low as 10−4 are detectable. These findings establish 3132
practical detection thresholds and observational biases that are 3133
critical for interpreting the demographics of debris disks in cur- 3134
rent and future HCI surveys.
3135
The connection between debris disks and planetary architec- 3136
tures was probed through dynamical modeling. In systems with 3137
resolved disks, we estimated the masses and semimajor axes of 3138
planets that could sculpt gaps or belt inner edges. For single-belt 3139
systems, planets capable of shaping the inner edges span masses 3140
from 0.1 to 13 MJup, with orbital radii consistent with observed 3141
disk edges. In double-belt systems, multiple planets with sub- 3142
Jovian masses can reproduce the gaps while remaining below 3143
current detection thresholds. These findings imply that Neptune- 3144
to sub-Saturn-mass planets at tens to hundreds of au may be 3145
common but remain undetected. The inferred planet populations 3146
align with those found by RV and transit surveys in the super- 3147
Earth to sub-Saturn range, though at larger orbital separations.
3148
Among the systems in our sample, only five, HD 39060 3149
(β Pic), HD 106906, HD 114082, HD 197481 (AU Mic), and 3150
HD 218396 (HR 8799), host both resolved debris disks and con- 3151
firmed planets. In HD 114082, we identify a second transiting 3152
giant planet candidate in TESS data, with a radius of 1.29 ± 3153
0.05 RJup and orbit near 1 au. Its misaligned orbit relative to the 3154
debris disk and the known planet, along with their close proxim- 3155
ity to the host star, well inside the typical formation region for gi- 3156
ant planets, suggests a history of dynamical evolution involving 3157
planet–planet scattering or planet migration. Continued monitor- 3158
ing of HD 114082 is required to refine the orbital parameters of 3159
both planets, particularly their eccentricities and the orientation 3160
of their orbital planes. These constraints are critical for assessing 3161
Article number, page 40 of 57


## Page 41


Engler et al.: Debris Disks demographics
the system’s long-term dynamical stability and for distinguish-
3162
ing between potential migration and scattering scenarios.
3163
Finally, we considered stellar companions. Few are found
3164
at separations overlapping the debris disks in our sample, con-
3165
sistent with selection biases excluding known multiples from
3166
SPHERE surveys. Nonetheless, stellar companions at 10−100 au
3167
are known to destabilize debris disks, likely explaining their
3168
scarcity in our resolved sample.
3169
This study presents the largest homogeneous analysis of de-
3170
bris disks imaged in scattered light with SPHERE, complement-
3171
ing previous surveys conducted with HST, Herschel, GPI, and
3172
ALMA. It is important to note, however, that our results are
3173
based on a sample that is inherently biased, as it primarily in-
3174
cludes targets selected for their high IR excess or previously
3175
known disk structures. This selection effect should be carefully
3176
considered when interpreting trends in disk properties and dust
3177
evolution. A more comprehensive and unbiased statistical as-
3178
sessment would require a broader sample, including fainter and
3179
lower-mass disks that remain undetected with current HCI tech-
3180
niques.
3181
Future studies will benefit from advancements in observa-
3182
tional capabilities, such as those provided by JWST, which
3183
can probe debris disks at mid-IR wavelengths (e.g., Boccaletti
3184
et al. 2024; Mâlin et al. 2024; Su et al. 2024), as well as next-
3185
generation ground-based instruments including ELT/METIS
3186
(Brandl et al. 2024). Additionally, combining high-resolution
3187
scattered-light imaging with ALMA millimeter observations
3188
will allow for a more complete picture of disk morphology,
3189
grain composition, and spatial segregation of dust populations.
3190
Expanding such multiwavelength approaches, together with ad-
3191
vancements in disk modeling techniques, is essential for refining
3192
our understanding of debris disk evolution and the architectures
3193
of planetary systems.

features within their respective debris disks. Figure B.1 shows

band data from three separate observing epochs. In this compos-

ite image, all three planetesimal belts are discernible, with the

ously detected in both HST and ALMA observations (Ren et al.

2021; Bayo et al. 2019).

Appendix C: Polarization fraction function of
3764
micron-sized dust particles
3765
To model the disk images of polarized scattered light, we
3766
adopted the pSPF incorporating a polarization fraction func-
3767
Fig. B.2: Total intensity image of the HD 16743 debris disk with
the H2H3 filter. The image is binned by 8×8 pixels and smoothed
using a Gaussian kernel with σ = 2 pixels. An extended feature
