tion derived under the Rayleigh scattering assumption (Eq 3). 3768
Rayleigh scattering describes the interaction of light with parti- 3769
cles significantly smaller than the wavelength of the incident ra- 3770
diation, and it exhibits a pronounced angular dependence, with 3771
maximum polarization occurring at scattering angle of 90◦. This 3772
approximation provides a useful analytic form for the polariza- 3773
tion behavior of small and compact grains in debris disks, al- 3774
though it becomes less accurate for larger or more complex dust 3775
particles.
3776
To illustrate this, we present in Fig. C.1 the polarization 3777
fraction functions measured for Mg-rich olivine samples with 3778
three different particle SDs alongside the theoretical Rayleigh 3779
scattering polarization fraction function for comparison. We se- 3780
lected olivine as a representative material because its presence is 3781
commonly inferred from cometary and debris disk spectra (e.g., 3782
Kolokolova & Jockers 1997; Chen et al. 2006). The measure- 3783
ments for samples with effective grain radii of 2.6 µm and 3.8 µm 3784
were conducted using a laser source at 633 nm (Muñoz et al. 3785
2000), while the data for the sample with an effective radius ex- 3786
ceeding 20 µm were obtained using a white light source and the 3787
spectral response of imaging polarimeter cameras in the wave- 3788
length range of 1.5 −1.6 µm (Renard et al. 2014)15.
3789
The scaled Rayleigh scattering function p(θ) is plotted in 3790
Fig. C.1 with a maximum polarization fraction pmax of 9%, cho- 3791
sen to match the measurement for the sample with an effec- 3792
tive grain radius of aeff = 3.8 µm. As shown, the overall shape 3793
of the Rayleigh polarization function reproduces the observed 3794
trend reasonably well. The largest discrepancy arises at large 3795
scattering angles (θ > 150◦), corresponding to small phase an- 3796
gles (phase angle = 180◦– scattering angle), where the mea- 3797
sured polarization fraction (< 4%) becomes negative, forming a 3798
so-called negative polarization branch. This sign inversion indi- 3799
cates a change in the orientation of the polarization vector from 3800
azimuthal to radial in the image of polarized intensity, meaning 3801

Fig. C.1: Polarization fraction measured for olivine dust sam-
ples with particle SDs characterized by different effective radii
(aeff), shown as a function of scattering angle. For comparison,
a Rayleigh scattering polarization fraction function with a max-
imum polarization pmax of 9% is overplotted (blue solid line).
Measurements for samples with aeff = 2.6 µm (blue circles)
and aeff = 3.8 µm (red diamonds) were obtained using a laser
source at 633 nm. Data for the sample with aeff > 20µm (yellow
crosses) were acquired using a white light source and camera
systems with a spectral response in the 1.5–1.6 µm range.
that the scattered light becomes preferentially polarized parallel
3802
to the scattering plane16 rather than perpendicular to it.
3803
The maximum polarization fraction of dust particles may
3804
also occur at scattering angles different from 90◦, though still
3805
typically close to it. For example, in Fig. C.1, the sample with
3806
an effective grain size of aeff = 2.6 µm exhibits a peak polariza-
3807
tion fraction at a scattering angle of approx. 100◦. In practice,
3808
the polarization fraction function of a debris disk, along with
3809
the SPF and pSPF, is expected to be smoothed due to averaging
3810
over a distribution of particle sizes and a mixture of dust popu-
3811
lations across different disk regions. We therefore conclude that
3812
the Rayleigh scattering function p(θ) offers a reasonable, though
3813
simplified, approximation that can be applied to model polari-
3814
metric images of debris disks. If a more accurate representation
3815
is required, the location of the maximum polarization fraction
3816
can be parametrized using a scaled version of the beta distribu-
3817
tion, as proposed by Ren et al. (2023). However, this approach
3818
introduces two additional degrees of freedom into the model.
3819

3820
and SD models

3822
els applying various approaches. Top row presents the MBB
3823
models consisting of one or two planetesimal rings. Bottom row
3824
shows the SD models based on the belt radii measured from the
3825
r2-scaled disk images.

light source (e.g., the star), the scattering particle, and the observer.

Fig. D.1: Examples of best-fit SED models using various approaches (see Sect. 5). The top row illustrates MBB models in which
the ring radius is a free parameter. In all cases, the disk SEDs were fitted with a single planetesimal ring, as shown in panel a for
BD 20-951 and panel b for HD 9672. For disks exhibiting a warm component, an additional BB component (Ring 2) representing
warm dust was included in the fit, as demonstrated in panel c for HD 9672 and panel d for HD 39060. The bottom row shows fits
using the SD model, where the ring radius is fixed to the value measured from the r2-scaled scattered-light images. If only one ring
is resolved, the SED is fitted with a single SD component, as in panel e for HD 112810. For systems with a warm dust component,
an additional BB component (Warm Component) was added, as illustrated in panel f for HD 112810. In cases where two cold belts
are resolved, the SED is fitted with two SD rings, as shown in panel g for HD 15115 and panel h for HD 131835.

Appendix F: Companions to the program stars
3835
Table F.1 presents the parameters of confirmed exoplanets with
3836
masses below 13 MJup in the stellar systems of our sample, as of
3837
August 1, 2024. The data were retrieved from the NASA Exo-
3838
planet archive17.
3839
To identify companions to the program stars with masses
3840
greater than 13 MJup, we followed the methodology outlined in
3841
Gratton et al. (2023b,a, 2024, 2025). Our search incorporated
3842
both direct observations of visual companions, primarily from
3843
Gaia data and HCI, and indirect evidence based on photometry
3844
(eclipsing binaries), RV measurements (spectroscopic binaries),
3845
and astrometry (mainly from Gaia).
3846
F.1. Visual binaries
3847
Wide visual companions (separation > 0.7′′) can be detected ei-
3848
ther as separate entries in Gaia DR3 or by HCI. Both are avail-
3849
able for all the program stars. Given the age of the stars, Gaia
3850
DR3 can reveal companions down to the star-brown dwarfs for
3851
separation larger than a few hundred au. HCI is sensitive to mas-
3852
sive planetary companions, but only in the separation range from
3853
a few tens to about 1000 au.
3854
We considered as physical companions all point sources that
3855
have similar parallax and proper motion, and are within 60′′ (that
3856
is, about 1000-10000 au, depending on distance of the target) of
3857
each of the program stars. We also notice that the astrometric
3858
solution may be missing for faint sources very close to brighter
3859
objects in the Gaia DR3 catalogue. Given the low-density of the
3860
background fields, we additionally considered as physical com-
3861
panions the objects listed in Gaia and projected within 2′′ of each
3862
star, but lacking an astrometric solution.
3863
F.2. Eclipsing binaries
3864
We searched for eclipsing binaries (EBs) within the TESS
3865
dataset but did not identify any among the program stars.
3866
F.3. Spectroscopic binaries
3867
We inspected the S B9 (Pourbaix et al. (2004), Tokovinin (2018))
3868
and Gaia DR3 binary Gaia Collaboration et al. (2023a) catalogs
3869
for spectroscopic and spectrophotometric binaries. In addition,
3870
we considered high-precision RV series from the literature and
3871
the low-precision one from Gaia DR3. Companions detected us-
3872
ing RVs typically have separation less than a few au’s.
3873
F.4. Astrometric binaries
3874
We inspected various catalogs looking for astrometric binaries
3875
based on Gaia data: nss_two_body_orbit (Gaia Collaboration
3876
et al. 2023a), nss_acceleration_astro (Gaia Collaboration et al.
3877
2023a, Holl et al. 2023).
3878
We considered proper motion acceleration (PMa) from
3879
Kervella et al. (2022). The PMa is the difference between the
3880
proper motion in Gaia DR3 (baseline of 34 months) and that
3881
determined using the position at Hipparcos (1991.25) and Gaia
3882
EDR3 (2016.0) epochs. This quantity is available for a vast ma-
3883
jority of the program stars. PMa is sensitive to binaries with a
3884
projected separation between 1 and 100 au. We consider any
3885
value of PMa with a S/N > 3 as an indication of the presence
3886
of companions.
3887
We also considered the re-normalised unit weight error
3888
(RUWE) as an indication of binarity. This parameter is an in-
3889
dication of the goodness of the 5-parameter solution found by
3890
Gaia (Lindegren et al. 2018). Belokurov et al. (2020) showed
3891
that when this parameter is > 1.4 the star is likely a binary, at
3892
17 www.exoplanetarchive.ipac.caltech.edu
least for stars that are not too bright (G > 4) and saturated in the 3893
Gaia scans. This method is sensitive to systems that have periods 3894
from a few months to a decade (Penoyre et al. 2022). The RUWE 3895
parameter is available for the vast majority of the program stars. 3896
F.5. Parameters for the components
3897
The semimajor axis and the mass for the companions are listed in 3898
Table F.2. They are obtained following the methods considered 3899
in Gratton et al. (2023b, 2024, 2025), briefly summarised in the 3900
following.
3901
For unresolved systems, the sum of the masses is made com- 3902
patible with the apparent G magnitude of the system, using the 3903
mass-luminosity relation for the Gaia G band appropriate for the 3904
age of the system. We assumed that the semimajor axis is equal 3905
to the projected separation divided by the parallax. On average, 3906
this corresponds to the thermal eccentricity distribution consid- 3907
ered by Ambartsumian (1937) of f(e) = 2e (see Brandeker et al. 3908
2006). Uncertainties in the masses derived using these recipes 3909
are small (well below 10%), while those for the semimajor axes 3910
are about 40% (see Figure A.1 in Brandeker et al. 2006).
3911
The indication of binarity for many objects comes from 3912
RUWE (> 1.4) or PMa (S/N > 3 objects) or a combination of 3913
these techniques. The secondary of these stars is not imaged, and 3914
no period or semimajor axis is determined. Since RV variations, 3915
RUWE, and PMa have different dependence on the semimajor 3916
axis and the mass, we may better constrain the parameters of the 3917
companions by combining different methods using all available 3918
information rather than considering only a single technique. We 3919
did this by means of exploring the semimajor axis – mass ratio 3920
plane using a Monte Carlo code. We assumed eccentric orbits, 3921
with uniform priors between 0 and 1 in eccentricity (which is in 3922
agreement with Hwang et al. 2022 for this range of separations), 3923
0 and 180 degrees in the angle of the ascending node Ω, and 0 3924
and 360 degrees in the periastron angle ω, and left the inclination 3925
and phase to assume a random value. In addition, the period was 3926
used to fix the solution whenever it was available. Uncertainties 3927
are large and only give order-of-magnitude estimates.
3928
